\mathrm{c}(N)

–

[k^{i},k^{j}]=0

General conformal

\mathrm{gc}(N)

–

\begin{aligned} {}[k^{k},m^{ij}]=&g^{ki}k^{j}-g^{kj}k^{i}\\
[k^{i},d]=&-k^{i}\\
[p^{i},k^{j}]=&2g^{ij}d-2m^{ij}\end{aligned}

The interpretation of these relations is straightforward. The first equation simply states that
m
generates a rotation algebra. The second equation and the absence of nonzero
\commutator{d}{m_{ab}}
indicate that
p^{\pm}
and
d
transform as a vector and a scalar under rotations respectively. The third equation shows that
p^{\pm}
behave like ladder operators with respect to the dilation
d
; and finally, the last equation gives their non-commutative nature.

2.2.2
Conformal group and its linear realizations

In section
2.2.1
we reviewed the conformal symmetry and the differential representation of the conformal algebra. By an exponential map, one can extend the analysis from the conformal algebra to the conformal group — or rather its connected component around the identity element. Indeed, this way, we can write the action of the conformal group under a
finite
transformation by starting with the infinitesimal transformation in eqn. (
2.2.6
); here, we simply present the final results:

\displaystyle P:

\displaystyle\;x^{i}\rightarrow x^{i}+a^{i}\;,

(2.2.15a)

\displaystyle M:

\displaystyle\;x^{i}\rightarrow e^{\theta_{ij}}x^{j}\;,

(2.2.15b)

\displaystyle D:

\displaystyle\;x^{i}\rightarrow e^{c}x^{i}\;,

(2.2.15c)

\displaystyle K:

\displaystyle\;x^{i}\rightarrow\frac{x^{i}-b^{i}x^{2}}{1-2b\cdot x+b^{2}x^{2}}\;.

(2.2.15d)

The transformation under the first three generators is fairly intuitive: we translate, rigidly rotate, or scale the coordinates. The last transformation on the other hand can be explained intuitively only through an
inversion
operation:

\displaystyle I:x^{i}\rightarrow\frac{x^{i}}{x^{2}}\;.

(2.2.16)

Even though this operator is not an element of the conformal group, conjugation by an inversion is actually the outer authomorphism of the conformal group and one can show that

\displaystyle K=-I\cdot P\cdot I\;.

(2.2.17)

In other words, the special conformal transformation in eqn. (
2.2.15d
) is simply an inversion followed by a translation followed by another inversion!

The nonlinear nature of the transformation in eqn. (
2.2.15d
) complicates the construction of the representations of the conformal group in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, there actually exists another space in which the realization of the conformal group is linear. To see that, we define the generators
M_{AB}

\displaystyle M_{ij}=

\displaystyle m_{ij}\;,

(2.2.18)

\displaystyle M_{0,-1}=

\displaystyle d\;,

\displaystyle M_{i,-1}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{i}^{+}+p_{i}^{-}\right)\;,

\displaystyle M_{i,0}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{i}^{-}-p_{i}^{+}\right)\;,

where
A=-1,0,1,\dots,d
and
i=1,\dots,d
. In terms of
M_{AB}
, we can rewrite the comutation relations in eqn. (
2.2.14
) as

\displaystyle\commutator{M_{AB}}{M_{CD}}=\left(g_{AD}M_{BC}+g_{BC}M_{DA}+g_{AC}M_{DB}+g_{BD}M_{AC}\right)

(2.2.19)

for the metric

\displaystyle g_{AB}\doteq\mathrm{diag}\left(-1,1,\dots,1\right)\;.

(2.2.20)

We realize that eqn. (
2.2.19
) is the commutation relations for the rotation algebra which indicates that the Euclidean group
\mathrm{gc}(d)
is isomorphic to an orthogonal algebra in
d+2
dimensions, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{gc}(d)\simeq\mathrm{so}(d+1,1)\;.

(2.2.21a)

Similarly, we can identify the algebra of the Lorentzian conformal algebra with another orthogonal algebra:

\displaystyle\mathrm{gc}(d-1,1)\simeq\mathrm{so}(d,2)\;.

(2.2.21b)

This isomorphism of the algebras suggests that we can work in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
(
\mathbb{R}^{d,2}
) instead of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
\mathbb{R}^{d-1,1}
) where the action of the conformal group is realized linearly. This
embedding space
approach was first suggested by Dirac, and has been extensively used ever since
[
159
,
157
,
153
]
. Below, we will quickly review this approach and illustrate how to construct invariant/covariant objects of the conformal group (such as correlation functions) via this approach.

The nonlinear action of the conformal group in eqn. (
2.2.15
) can be mapped to the linear action of the special orthogonal group in the embedding space, once the extra degrees of freedom are taken care of: this is achieved by mapping
null rays
in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
to points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The intuitive explanation for this is that null rays are the objects in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
that are mapped to themselves under the action of
\mathrm{so}(d+1,1)
— similar to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
being mapped to itself under
\mathrm{gc}(d)
— and that they have the correct degrees of freedom.

A straightforward procedure to map from the embedding space back to the physical space is by
fixing the gauge
of the freedom
X_{A}\sim\lambda X_{A}
; a common choice is the
Poincaré
section where we scale
X_{A}
to
P_{a}
defined as

\displaystyle P_{A}\coloneqq(1,x^{2},x_{i})

(2.2.22)

in the lightcone coordinate
X_{A}=\left(X_{+},X_{-},X_{i}\right)
for
X_{\pm}=X_{0}\pm X_{-1}
. With this choice, we see that
\mathrm{so}(d+1,1)
invariants map as

\displaystyle X_{A}Y^{A}\quad\rightarrow\quad-\frac{1}{2}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}\;.

(2.2.23)

Similarly, one can show that
\mathrm{so}(d+1,1)
tensors map as

\displaystyle T_{A_{1}\cdots A_{\ell}}(X^{A}_{1},\dots)\quad\rightarrow\quad t_{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}(P^{A}_{1},\dots)\equiv\frac{\partial P^{A_{1}}}{\partial x^{a_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial P^{A_{\ell}}}{\partial x^{a_{\ell}}}T_{A_{1}\cdots A_{\ell}}(X^{A}_{1},\dots)\;.

(2.2.24)

We can also write down a prescription to map fermionic representations of
\mathrm{so}(d+1,1)
but it is actually far easier to work in an
index-free
fashion where auxiliary spinors and auxiliary vectors are used in the embedding space to construct
\mathrm{so}(d+1,1)
invariants. However, before illustrating that, we will briefly discuss the stabilizer subgroup of the conformal group to review the labeling of the operators in the irreducible representations.

The stabilizer subalgebra
h
of the conformal algebra consists of the generators
m_{ij},\;p_{i}^{-},\;d
if we choose it to fix the point
x=0
. From eqn. (
2.2.14
) we then see that

\displaystyle h=\left(\mathrm{c}(N)\oplus_{s}\mathrm{d}(1)\right)\oplus_{s}\mathrm{so}(N)\;.

(2.2.25)

We realize from eqn. (
2.2.14c
) that
p_{i}^{-}
acts a lowering operator for
d
, hence we can define
primary operator
which satisfies

\displaystyle\commutator{p_i^-}{\cO(0)}=0\;,

(2.2.26)

which is in the highest weight representation of
\mathrm{d}(1)
. As these operators are invariant under the action of
c(N)
,
18
18

18

Under the action of
c(N)
,
\mathcal{O}(0)
transforms as
e^{-b^{i}p^{-}_{i}}\mathcal{O}(0)e^{b^{i}p^{-}_{i}}=\mathcal{O}(0)+b^{i}[\mathcal{O}(0),p^{-}_{i}]+\dots=\mathcal{O}(0)
by the Hausdorff formula.
the stabilizer algebra for primary operators is effectively
\mathrm{d}(1)\oplus_{s}\mathrm{so}(N)
for which the operators are labeled by a continuous parameter
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}
for
\mathrm{d}(1)
and a representation
\rho\in\mathrm{so}(d)
for the conformal algebra in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. When we map from algebra to the group, we need to take the fermionic representations into account as well, hence we denote

\displaystyle\text{primary operators:}\quad\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\rho}\quad\text{with}\quad\Delta\in\mathbb{R}\;,\quad\rho\in\mathrm{Spin}(d)\;.

(2.2.27a)

As eqn. (
2.2.14c
) indicates that
p^{+}_{i}
is a raising operator for
d
, operators
p^{+}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{O}
,
p^{+}_{i}\cdot p^{+}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{O}
, and so on are not primary operators. Such operators are called
descendant operators
and are given by eqn. (
2.2.11a
) as

\displaystyle\text{descendant operators:}\quad\frac{\partial^{n}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\rho}}{\partial x^{\mu_{1}}\dots\partial x^{\mu_{n}}}\quad\text{with}\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\text{ and }\mathcal{O}=\text{primary}\;.

(2.2.27b)

With the little detour of the labeling of operators under the conformal group finished, we can get back to their construction in the embedding space, and in particular, the index-free construction of conformal correlators which bypass technical computations in eqn. (
2.2.24
). Without dwelling on the details, we will simply review the procedure

•

For the bosonic representations in any
d
, introduce an auxiliary vector
Z_{A}
for each row of the Young diagram of the representation
\rho
for the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\rho}
. In
d=3
, we can instead introduce a single auxiliary spinor
S_{I}
for both bosonic and fermionic representations. In
d=4
, we instead introduce two auxiliary spinors
S_{I}
and
\bar{S}_{I}
for all representations.

•

We construct the most general function of the auxiliary vectors/spinors and position vectors which satisfy following constraints:

1.

Invariant under the action of
\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1)
.

2.

Homogeneous in both the auxiliary vectors/spinors and position vectors with degrees of homogeneity fixed for a given representation.

3.

Restricted to the null cone
X^{2}=0
.

4.

Transverse, i.e. position and auxilary vector/spinor are orthogonal. In
3d
, this means
S_{I}X^{I}_{\;J}=0
.
19
19

19

Here
X^{I}_{\;J}
is the position vector written in spinor indices.

•

The resultant expression is projected to the Poincaré section by
X_{A}\rightarrow P_{A}
for
P_{A}
in eqn. (
2.2.22
) and by projecting auxiliary vectors/spinors accordingly. For example, in
3d
we project the
5d
spinor
S_{I}
as

\displaystyle S_{I}\rightarrow\begin{pmatrix}s_{\alpha}\\
x^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}s^{\beta}\end{pmatrix}

(2.2.28)

in terms of the position
x^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\equiv x_{\mu}(\gamma^{\mu})^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
and the
3d
spinor
s_{\alpha}
.

We can illustrate this procedure for the construction of the two point function
\expectationvalue{\cO(x_1,s_1)\cO(x_2,s_2)}
in
3d
, where we use the shorthand notation

\displaystyle{A\ell oca\ell operatorinthegenericrepresentationoftherotationsubgroupofconforma\ell group}(x,s)\coloneqq s_{\alpha_{1}}\dots s_{\alpha_{2\ell}}\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{2\ell}}(x)

(2.2.29)

for the spinor indices
\alpha_{i}
. In embedding space, we are then trying to construct
\expectationvalue{\cO(X_1,S_1)\cO(X_2,S_2)}
which is homogeneous in
X_{i}
and
S_{i}
with degrees
-\Delta-\ell
and 2Spin of a local operator in three spacetime dimensions. We then write down the only such
\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1)
invariant:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO(X_1,S_1)\cO(X_2,S_2)}=c\frac{\left((S_{1})_{I}(S_{2})^{I}\right)^{2\ell}}{\left((X_{1})_{A}(X_{2})^{A}\right)^{\Delta+\ell}}\;,

(2.2.30)

where we note that
(S_{1})_{I}(X_{1})^{I}_{\;J}(S_{2})^{J}=(S_{1})_{I}(X_{2})^{I}_{\;J}(S_{2})^{J}=0
by the transverness condition. With eqn. (
2.2.23
) and eqn. (
2.2.28
), we then obtain

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO(x_1,s_2)\cO(x_2,s_2)}\propto\frac{\left[(s_{1})_{\alpha}(x_{12})^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}(s_{2})^{\beta}\right]^{2\ell}}{x_{12}^{2\Delta+2\ell}}\;.

(2.2.31)

2.3
Lightning review of the conformal bootstrap

In this section, we will briefly review the historical philosophy of the conformal bootstrap and its modern applications in practice.

As we noted in section
2.1.1
, the bootstrap approach historically bypasses
constructionism
: one starts with some fundamental conditions and derive the theory with a bottom-up approach, using these conditions as constraints on the landscape of the theory. Conformal symmetry by itself is sufficient to fix the form of two and three point correlation functions, and one aims to use other conditions such as unitarity and operator product expansion associativity to entirely determine the form of all correlation functions, importantly, without resorting to any input from a microscopic theory.

This method is in stark contrast to the traditional top-down approach where one usually starts a theory by writing down a Lagrangian. This is an old habit of physicists and it served them extremely well for the past few centuries. However there is an important philosophical difference between the Lagrangian approach and that of the bootstrap: starting in the UV by writing a Lagrangian, one inherently assumes that physics is
constructive
, i.e. one can not only reduce all physics to some fundamental laws and particles but also obtain useful information regarding phenomena observed in the IR by starting with fundamental laws and particles in the UV and by
constructing their way back
. In contrast, the main objects of the conformal bootstrap are correlation functions, hence there is no need for constructionism as one already works with the theoretically closest objects to the observables.

Despite this philosophical difference between Lagrangian and bootstrap approaches, most physicists including the bootstrappers already accept both a reductionist and a constructionist approach towards nature --- and hence combine the bootstrap approach with the Lagrangian theories --- and we will assume in the rest of the thesis that both top-down and bottom-up approaches are equivalent in this sense.
20
20

20

Interested reader can refer to the infamous paper of Anderson
[
214
]
and a more recent relevant talk,
27^{\text{th}}
Occam lecture of Slava Rychkov — see
https://www.merton.ox.ac.uk/event/27th-ockham-lecture-reductionism-vs-bootstrap-are-things-big-always-made-things-elementary
.

Let us switch gears and review the modern employment of the conformal bootstrap; for simplicity, we will consider the analytic bootstrap for scalar 4-point functions
\expectationvalue{\phi_1\phi_2\phi_3\phi_4}
, which take the general form

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\ac{phi}_1(x_1)\phi_2(x_2)\phi_3(x_3)\phi_4(x_4)}=\left(\frac{x_{23}}{x_{13}}\right)^{\Delta_{12}}\left(\frac{x_{13}}{x_{14}}\right)^{\Delta_{43}}\frac{G(z,\bar{z})}{x_{12}^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}x_{34}^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}}\;,

(2.3.1)

where
x_{ij}=x_{i}-x_{j}
and
\Delta_{ij}=\Delta_{i}-\Delta_{j}
, with Scaling dimension of a local operator:
\mathcal{O}_{i}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{-\Delta_{i}}\mathcal{O}_{i}(x)
i
being the scaling dimension of
\phi_{i}
. The conformal cross-ratios (Holomorphic cross ratio of four points in the standard conformal frame,Anti-holomorphic cross ratio of four points in the standard conformal frame) are given by

z\overline{z}=\frac{x_{12}^{2}x_{34}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\quad,\quad(1-z)(1-\overline{z})=\frac{x_{14}^{2}x_{23}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}x_{24}^{2}}\;.

(2.3.2)

The function
G(z,\bar{z})
can be expanded in conformal blocks for the
\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\rightarrow\phi_{3}\phi_{4}
OPE as

\displaystyle G(z,\bar{z})=\sum_{\mathcal{O}}f_{12\mathcal{O}}f_{43\mathcal{O}}g_{h,\bar{h}}^{r,s}(z,\bar{z})\;,

(2.3.3)

where we have introduced the variables

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}{2}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle s=\frac{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}{2}\;,

(2.3.4a)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta-\ell}{2}\;,

\displaystyle\bar{h}=\frac{\Delta+\ell}{2}\;,

(2.3.4b)

where
\Delta
and
\ell
are the scaling dimension and spin of the exchanged operator. This parameterization is convenient for the lightcone expansion, where
\overline{h}
is the natural expansion parameter.

For a spacelike configuration of the operators, we have
\expectationvalue{\phi_1\phi_2\phi_3\phi_4}=\expectationvalue{\phi_3\phi_2\phi_1\phi_4}

which with eqn. (
2.3.1
) and eqn. (
2.3.3
) indicate

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{O}\in\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2}}f_{12\mathcal{O}}f_{43\mathcal{O}}\frac{g_{h,\bar{h}}^{r,s}(z,\bar{z})}{\left((1-z)(1-\overline{z})\right)^{(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3})/2}}=\sum_{\mathcal{O}\in\phi_{3}\times\phi_{2}}f_{32\mathcal{O}}f_{41\mathcal{O}}\frac{g_{h,\bar{h}}^{r,s}(1-z,1-\bar{z})}{(z\overline{z})^{(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3})/2}}\;.

(2.3.5)

This is the crossing equation: it constraints the dynamical data — OPE coefficient
f
’s — in
\mathbb{C}
spanned by
z,\overline{z}
where the conformal blocks,
g(z,\overline{z})
, are theory-independent kinematic objects.

In general dimensions the conformal blocks are not known in a simple closed form,
21
21

21

See
[
17
]
for a review of various methods to calculate the blocks.
but in the limit where two operators become light-like separated they display a universal behavior which makes analytic results possible. In terms of the conformal cross-ratios, if we take the limit
z\rightarrow 0
, then the leading order behavior of conformal blocks in any dimension is

g_{h,\overline{h}}^{r,s}(z,\overline{z})\underset{z\rightarrow 0}{\simeq}z^{h}\overline{z}^{\overline{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\hb-r,\hb+s}{2\hb};\overline{z}\biggr]\;,

(2.3.6)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}
is the standard hypergeometric function. This approximation is sufficient to compute the leading large-
\ell
corrections to the spectrum of double-twist operators using analytic bootstrap techniques, as was first demonstrated in
[
112
,
111
]
.

In this thesis we will be interested in higher order corrections in the large-
\ell
expansion
[
115
,
119
,
126
]
, hence we will need subleading terms in the above expansion. In three dimensions, one can use dimensional reduction to expand the conformal block in terms of the 2d conformal blocks
[
215
]
, or equivalently use an
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
expansion
[
126
]
. These two methods are equivalent since the 2d blocks are a simple combination of 1d, or
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, blocks.

The conformal blocks in any dimension can be expanded as

\displaystyle g_{h,\overline{h}}^{r,s}(z,\overline{z})={}

\displaystyle{}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{n}A_{n,j}^{r,s}(h,\overline{h})z^{h+n}k^{r,s}_{2(\bar{h}+j)}(\bar{z})\;,

(2.3.7a)

\displaystyle k^{r,s}_{2\bar{h}}(\bar{z})\coloneqq{}

\displaystyle{}\overline{z}^{\overline{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\hb-r,\hb+s}{2\hb};\overline{z}\biggr]\;,

(2.3.7b)

where
k^{r,s}_{2\bar{h}}(z)
is the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
block. Using the decomposition mentioned above, or by solving the Casimir differential equation, the first two levels are straightforward to work out and are given by

\displaystyle A^{r,s}_{0,0}(h,\overline{h})

\displaystyle=1\;,

(2.3.8a)

\displaystyle A^{r,s}_{1,-1}(h,\overline{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{h-\bar{h}}{2h-2\bar{h}+1}\;,

(2.3.8b)

\displaystyle A^{r,s}_{1,0}(h,\overline{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{rs\left(h-2\bar{h}^{2}+2\bar{h}-1\right)}{(2h-1)(\bar{h}-1)\bar{h}}-h-r+s\right)\;,

(2.3.8c)

\displaystyle A^{r,s}_{1,1}(h,\overline{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{(h+\bar{h}-1)(\bar{h}-r)(\bar{h}+r)(\bar{h}-s)(\bar{h}+s)}{4\bar{h}^{2}(2\bar{h}-1)(2\bar{h}+1)(2h+2\bar{h}-1)}\;,

(2.3.8d)

for
d=3
. By using such an expansion one can solve eqn. (
2.3.5
) order by order in
z
.

Chapter 3
Fermions in Conformal Field Theories: A Perturbative Approach

3.1
Fermions in
d=3

There are important and relevant
3d
CFTs with fermionic operators such as the
3d
supersymmetric Ising and Gross-Neveu-Yukawa model – a scalar field coupled to the Gross-Neveu model via Yukawa interactionss, and they are invoked to explain several phenomena such as the phase transitions in graphene and the time-reversal symmetry breaking in d-wave superconductors. We refer to
[
17
]
for details; we’ll only briefly review Gross-Neveu-Yukawa model – a scalar field coupled to the Gross-Neveu model via Yukawa interactions below as an example.

The Gross-Neveu-Yukawa model – a scalar field coupled to the Gross-Neveu model via Yukawa interactions can be described by the action
[
216
,
217
,
218
]

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d}x\left(\bar{\psi}_{i}(\not{\partial}+g\phi){A\ell oca\ell spin-\frac{1}{2}operatorofconforma\ell groupin\mathbb{R}^{2,1}}^{i}+\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi+m^{2}\phi^{2}+\lambda\phi^{4}\right)

(3.1.1)

for the flavor index
i=1,\dots,N
. In
d=4-\epsilon
, one can show by working perturbatively at large
N
that this theory has an IR fixed point which coincides with the UV fixed point of the Gross-Neveu model – the theory of
N
fermions with quartic interaction
[
219
,
220
]
. This is similar to the familiar relation between the Wilson- Fisher fixed points of the
\phi^{4}
theory and the UV fixed points of the non-linear sigma model in
d=2+\epsilon

[
221
]
. The critical point is believed to survive up to
\epsilon=1
(hence
d=3
), where large
N
studies have been carried out
[
222
,
223
,
224
]
. Reference
[
225
]
furthermore studies the conformal OPE expansion alongside the large
N
expansion of this model.

The numerical bootstrap has addressed fermions in
d=3
— along with the contact to GNY models — in
[
81
]
and
[
226
]
: we refer the reader to those papers for further details.

3.1.1
Notations & Conventions

In this thesis, we follow the same conventions with
[
1
]
for
3d
fermions, so we will be brief here. We use the Minkowski metric in mostly plus signature
\eta_{\mu\nu}=\text{diag}(-1,1,1)
, and as our focus are fermions we will be considering the double cover of the
3d
conformal group
\mathrm{SO}(3,2)
, which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
. We will hence use representations of the symplectic group; in particular, the smallest fundamental representation will act on a real two dimensional vector space, describing Majorana fermions.

The conformal group
\mathrm{SO}(3,2)
can be realized linearly in two higher dimensions as we reviewed in section
2.2.2
, therefore we embed
\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
representations as the projective null representations of
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
in this 5d embedding space, use the fact that conformal symmetry acts as linear isometries in this formalism, and project back to physical structures by fixing the extra degrees of freedom in the embedding space.

In practice, one removes the extra degrees of freedom of the embedding space by going to the so-called Poincaré section,

\displaystyle X^{A}\rightarrow(x^{\mu},1,x^{2})\;,

(3.1.2)

where we are working in the lightcone coordinates
X^{A}=(X^{\mu},X^{+},X^{-})
and
X^{\pm}
are related to the Cartesian coordinates as
X^{\pm}=X^{4}\pm X^{3}
. One can reverse this projection and lift any point to the embedding space in this Poincaré section.
1
1

1

The only exception is the point
\infty
: one cannot use the same Poincaré section
(x^{\mu},1,x^{2})
used for finite points for the point at infinity as well. A simple way to see this is as follows. We start with
X^{a}=(x^{\mu},X^{+},X^{-})
and impose nullness to obtain
X^{A}=(x^{\mu},X^{+},x^{2}/X^{+})
. If we now consider an inversion as
x^{\mu}_{R}=x^{\mu}/x^{2}
, we see that
X^{A}_{R}=(x^{\mu}/x^{2},X^{+},(x^{2}X^{+})^{-1})\sim(x^{\mu},x^{2}X^{+},1/X^{+})
where we use projectiveness of the representation. For
x^{2}\neq 0
, we can choose
X^{+}=1/x^{2}
for
X_{R}^{A}
and
X^{+}=1
for
X^{A}
, which means we can write both
x^{\mu}
and
x_{R}^{\mu}
using the same Poincaré section
(x^{\mu},1,x^{2})
. For
x^{2}=0
, this is no longer possible, and the reflected point
x_{R}^{\mu}=x^{\mu}/x^{2}=\infty
should be instead in the Poincaré section
(x^{\mu},x^{2},1)
.

As developed in
[
153
]
and generalized to 3d spinors in
[
81
]
, one can encode spinors by polynomials with auxiliary spinor fields:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(x,s)={}

\displaystyle s_{\alpha_{1}}s_{\alpha_{2}}\dots s_{\alpha_{2\ell}}\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{2\ell}}(x)\;,

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{2\ell}}(x)={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\ell)!}\frac{\partial^{2\ell}}{\partial_{s_{a_{1}}}\partial_{s_{a_{2}}}\dots\partial_{s_{a_{2\ell}}}}\mathcal{O}(x,s)\;.

In this formalism, we can relate the spinor field
\Psi(X,S)\coloneqq S_{I}\Psi^{I}(X)
in the embedding space and the spinor field
\psi(x,s)\coloneqq s_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}(x)
in the physical space as

\Psi(X,S)=\frac{1}{(X^{+})^{\Delta_{\psi}}}\psi(x,s)\;,

(3.1.4)

where
S_{I}
is taken to the Poincaré section via setting

S_{I}=\sqrt{X^{+}}\begin{pmatrix}s_{\alpha}\\
x^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}s^{\beta}\end{pmatrix}\;.

(3.1.5)

Here we use the matrices
\gamma
and
\Gamma
to convert the indices,

X^{I}_{\;\;J}\equiv X^{A}(\Gamma_{A})^{I}_{\;\;J}\;,\qquad x^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}\equiv x^{\mu}(\gamma_{\mu})^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}\;.

(3.1.6)

The
3d
gamma matrices and
\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
invariant
\epsilon
are defined as

\displaystyle(\gamma_{0})^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}=i(\sigma_{2})_{\alpha\beta}\;,\quad(\gamma_{1})^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}=(\sigma_{1})_{\alpha\beta}\;,\quad(\gamma_{2})^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}=(\sigma_{3})_{\alpha\beta}\;,\quad\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}=i(\sigma_{2})_{\alpha\beta}\;,

(3.1.7)

where
\sigma_{i}
are the standard Pauli matrices:

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad\sigma_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-i\\
i&0\\
\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad\sigma_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{array}\right).

The embedding space gamma matrices and
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
invariant
\Omega
are then defined as:
2
2

2

We note that gamma matrices are defined such that the generators of
\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
are given as
-\frac{i}{4}[\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}]
and
-\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma^{A},\Gamma^{B}]
, respectively.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}=\gamma_{2}\otimes\gamma_{0}\;,\quad\Gamma_{1}=\mathbbm{I}\otimes\gamma_{1}\;,\quad\Gamma_{2}=\mathbbm{I}\otimes\gamma_{2}\;,\quad\Gamma_{3}=\gamma_{0}\otimes\gamma_{0}\;,\quad\Gamma_{4}=\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{0}\;,\quad\Omega=\epsilon\otimes\mathbbm{I}

(3.1.9)

with the embedding space metric
g_{IJ}=\text{diag}\left(-,+,+,+,-\right)
. In lightcone coordinates, the gamma matrices take the form

\displaystyle(\Gamma^{\mu})^{I}_{\;\;J}=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\gamma^{\mu}\right)^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}&0\\
0&\left(\gamma^{\mu}\right)_{\alpha}^{\;\;\beta}\end{pmatrix}\;,\quad(\Gamma^{+})^{I}_{\;\;J}=\begin{pmatrix}0&2\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\;,\quad(\Gamma^{-})^{I}_{\;\;J}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
2\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(3.1.10)

Finally, we raise (lower) the spinor indices with
\mathrm{SP}
invariants by acting from right (left), e.g.
x_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}x^{\beta}
and
x^{\alpha}=x_{\beta}\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}
.

To describe the embedding space spinor structures, we define the shorthand notation

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{S_1X_2X_3\dots X_{n-1}S_n}\equiv

\displaystyle-S_{1}\cdot X_{2}\cdot X_{3}\cdots X_{n-1}\cdot S_{n}

(3.1.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(S_{1})_{I}(X_{2})^{I}_{\;\;J}(X_{3})^{J}_{\;\;K}\cdots(X_{n-1})^{L}_{\;\;M}(S_{n})^{M}.

In this convention, we diagonalize the spectrum and normalize the operators
\mathcal{O}^{\Delta,\ell}
such that they satisfy

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO^{\De,l}(X_1,S_1)\cO^{\De,l}(X_2,S_2)}=i^{2\ell}\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1S_2}^{2\ell}}{X_{12}^{2\Delta+2\ell}}.

(3.1.12)

For example, two-point functions of fermions in embedding and physical spaces read as

\expectationvalue{\Psi(X_1,S_1)\Psi(X_2,S_2)}=i\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1S_2}}{X_{12}^{\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}}}\;,\qquad\expectationvalue{\psi^\alpha(x_1)\psi_\beta(x_2)}=i\frac{(x_{12})^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}}{x_{12}^{2\Delta_{\psi}+1}}\;.

(3.1.13)

Conformal symmetry also fixes the form of three point correlators albeit non-uniquely unless two of the operators are scalars. Therefore, in general, one writes down there point correlator
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}
in terms of three point structures
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}^{(i)}
as

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}=\sum\limits_{i}\lambda^{i}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}^{(i)}\;,

(3.1.14)

where
\lambda
are the OPE coefficients. We stress that
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}^{(i)}
are
not
physical three point correlator; in particular, they are formal expressions and do not necessarily satisfy Fermi-statistics if operators are interchanged. Throughout this paper, we will refer to these objects as three-point structures, for which we will follows the conventions of
[
81
,
148
]
and choose three point structures as:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\phi_1\phi_2\cO_3}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\expectationvalue{S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell)/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell)/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell)/2}},

(3.1.15a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_1\phi_2\cO_3}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1S_3}\expectationvalue{ S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell-\frac{1}{2})/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell-\frac{1}{2})/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell+\frac{1}{2})/2}},

(3.1.15b)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_1\phi_2\cO_3}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1X_2S_3}\expectationvalue{ S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell+\frac{1}{2})/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell+\frac{1}{2})/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell-\frac{1}{2})/2}},

(3.1.15c)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_1\psi_2\cO_3}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1S_2}\expectationvalue{ S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell+1)/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell)/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell)/2}},

(3.1.15d)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_1\psi_2\cO_3}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1S_3}\expectationvalue{S_2S_3}\expectationvalue{ S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell-1}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell-1)/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell)/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell)/2}},

(3.1.15e)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_1\psi_2\cO_3}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(X_{23}\expectationvalue{S_1S_3}\expectationvalue{S_2X_1S_3}+X_{13}\expectationvalue{S_2S_3}\expectationvalue{S_1X_2S_3}\right)\expectationvalue{ S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell-1}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell-1)/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell)/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell)/2}},

(3.1.15f)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_1\psi_2\cO_3}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(X_{23}\expectationvalue{S_1S_3}\expectationvalue{S_2X_1S_3}-X_{13}\expectationvalue{S_2S_3}\expectationvalue{S_1X_2S_3}\right)\expectationvalue{ S_3X_1X_2S_3}^{\ell-1}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}+\ell-1)/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell)/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell)/2}},

(3.1.15g)

where we define for brevity

\displaystyle X_{ab}\coloneqq-2X_{a}\cdot X_{b}\;,

(3.1.16a)

and

\displaystyle{Shorthandnotationforthefrequent\ell yusedcombination\Delta_{a}+\Delta_{b}-\Delta_{c}}\coloneqq\Delta_{a}+\Delta_{b}-\Delta_{c}\;.

(3.1.16b)

For integer spin we can also convert to vector notation,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{2J}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{J}}\gamma_{\mu_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots\gamma_{\mu_{J}}^{\alpha_{2J-1}\alpha_{2J}},

(3.1.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{J}}=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{J}\gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{J}}_{\alpha_{2J-1}\alpha_{2J}}\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{2J}},

where
\mathcal{O}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{J}}
is a symmetric traceless tensor. If we introduce the auxiliary polarization vectors
z_{\mu}
, and insist that

\displaystyle z_{\mu_{1}}\dots z_{\mu_{\ell}}\mathcal{O}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{\ell}}=s_{\alpha_{1}}\dots s_{\alpha_{2\ell}}\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{2\ell}}

(3.1.18)

holds, we find the following relation:

\displaystyle z_{\mu}=s_{\alpha}s_{\beta}\gamma^{\alpha\beta}.

(3.1.19)

We can now use these relations to convert two and three-point functions to vector notation for integer spin. In particular, eqn. (
3.1.12
) and eqn. (
3.1.15a
) become

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO^{\De,l}(X_1,Z_1)\cO^{\De,l}(X_2,Z_2)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\frac{H_{12}^{\ell}}{X_{12}^{\Delta_{\ell}}},

(3.1.20a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\f(X_1,Z_1)\f(X_2,Z_2)\cO(X_3,Z_3)}=

\displaystyle\frac{V_{3}^{\ell}}{X_{12}^{(\Delta_{123}-\ell)/2}X_{23}^{(\Delta_{231}+\ell)/2}X_{31}^{(\Delta_{312}+\ell)/2}},

(3.1.20b)

where we define

\displaystyle H_{12}\equiv{}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-2\left[(Z_{1}\cdot Z_{2})(X_{1}\cdot X_{2})-(X_{1}\cdot Z_{2})(Z_{1}\cdot X_{2})\right],

(3.1.21)

\displaystyle V_{3}\equiv{}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\frac{(Z_{3}\cdot X_{1})(X_{2}\cdot X_{3})-(Z_{3}\cdot X_{2})(X_{1}\cdot X_{3})}{X_{1}\cdot X_{2}},

in the conventions of
[
153
]
.

Associated to every representation
\mathcal{O}
is a shadow representation
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
which has dimension
\widetilde{\Delta}
and the same
\mathrm{SO}(d)
representation
\rho
3
3

3

In even dimensions we actually need the reflected representation
\rho^{R}
, but in odd dimensions the two are equivalent so to simplify the discussion we will ignore the distinction.
where we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}=d-\Delta

(3.1.22)

for brevity. Then there exists a conformally-invariant pairing:

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger},\mathcal{O}\right)=\int d^{d}x\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2J}}(x)\mathcal{O}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2J}}(x),

(3.1.23)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}
has dimension
\widetilde{\Delta}
. We will work with real operators, so we can drop the
\dagger
in the expressions which follow.

In the rest of the thesis we will implicitly contract indices when an operator and its shadow are being integrated over, we will always use spinor indices, and suppressed spinor indices always go from southwest to northeast. In particular, this means that order of correlators in an expression matters in our conventions, for instance

\displaystyle\int dx\expectationvalue{\cO_1 \cO(x) \cO_2 }\expectationvalue{\cO_3 \tl\cO(x) \cO_4 }=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}}}\int dx\expectationvalue{\cO_3 \tl\cO(x) \cO_4 }\expectationvalue{\cO_1 \cO(x) \cO_2 }\ .

(3.1.24)

By using the conformally-invariant pairing in eqn. (
3.1.23
), we can define
shadow transformation
which maps a representation
\mathcal{O}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}](x)\equiv\int dy\mathcal{O}(y)\expectationvalue{\tl\cO(y)\tl\cO(x)}.

(3.1.25)

We will adopt the convention that the shadow transform always acts by multiplying the two-point function from the left. The distinction is immaterial for bosonic correlators, but when we introduce fermions the ordering does matter.

3.1.2
Double twist sector of
3d
fermions

It is shown in
[
111
]
that every CFT has a double twist sector which asymptottes to Mean Field Theory operators at large spin. This spectrum is important for various reasons; for example, it is an open question whether several relevant operators such as stress tensor lie in these trajectories. Likewise, these operators correspond to two particle states in the bulk via the AdS/CFT correspondence. Finally, double twist operators are the relevant spectrum in a channel to reproduce low lying operators such as exchange of the identity operator and stress tensor in the crossed channel in the light-cone limit.

In the free theory limit, double twist operators can be written down as composite operators of the form

\displaystyle:A_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{p}}\partial_{\beta_{11}\beta_{12}}\cdots\partial_{\beta_{q1}\beta_{q2}}(\partial^{2})^{r}B_{\rho_{1}\dots\rho_{s}}:-\text{ total derivatives}\;,

(3.1.26)

where total derivative parts are extracted from the normal-ordered product to ensure that the resultant operator is a primary. In addition, the open indices above are symmetrized or antisymmetrized (contracted) dependent on the representation.

We group such operators into towers which has the same twist
\tau=\Delta-\ell
. In particular, we observe that inclusion of a partial derivative with open indices does not change the twist hence we define and denote the double twist towers as

\displaystyle{}[AB]_{n}\xrightarrow{\text{free theory limit}}\{A(\partial^{2})^{n}B,\;A\partial_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}(\partial^{2})^{n}B,\;A\partial_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\partial_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}(\partial^{2})^{n}B,\;\cdots\}\;,

(3.1.27)

where
A
and
B
may have open or contracted indices. In a free theory, the double twist tower
[AB]_{n}
is a collection of composite operators schematically given in the equation above
4
4

4

We emphasize that the expression
A(\partial^{2})^{n}B
and so on are schematic as the correct form should be normal-ordered with total derivative pieces removed as defined in eqn. (
3.1.26
).
and all these operators have the same twist
\tau_{[AB]_{n}}=\tau_{A}+\tau_{B}+2n
. Once we move away from the free theory, we can no longer describe double twist operators with such simple composite structures; nevertheless, they are still in the spectrum and they satisfy

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\tau_{[AB]_{n}}=\tau_{A}+\tau_{B}+2n\;,

(3.1.28)

hence we can define a double twist tower as a collection of operators with the same twist accumulation point as spin goes to infinity. At finite spin, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{[AB]_{n}}=\tau_{A}+\tau_{B}+2n+\gamma_{[AB]_{n}}(n,\ell)\;,

(3.1.29)

where
\gamma
is the
anomalous dimension
. One of the successes of the lightcone bootstrap was to show that for any CFT
5
5

5

We restrict ourselves to local CFTs with twist gap. In
d=3
, any unitary CFT does have the required twist gap.

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\ell^{\tau_{0}}\gamma_{[AB]_{n}}(n,\ell)=\text{ constant}\;,

(3.1.30)

where
\tau_{0}
is the twist of lowest twist non-identy operator in the spectrum of the crossed channel, which is usually the stress tensor. For example, we expect a double twist family

\displaystyle{}[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}\xrightarrow{\text{free theory limit}}\{\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha},\;\psi_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta_{11}\beta_{12}}\psi^{\alpha},\;\psi_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta_{11}\beta_{12}}\partial_{\beta_{21}\beta_{22}}\psi^{\alpha},\;\cdots\}

(3.1.31)

in the spectrum of fermionic CFTs: one aim of the conformal bootstrap is to find an analytic form for the anomalous dimension of this tower.

Let’s now step back and briefly discuss what kind of representations we expect for double twist operators for
3d
CFTs. We know that the irreducible representations of the
\mathrm{so}(3)
algebra are labeled by one index
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where tensor products of two irreps have the well known reduction

\displaystyle j_{1}\otimes j_{2}=(j_{1}+j_{2})\oplus(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)\oplus(j_{1}+j_{2}-2)\oplus\cdots\oplus\absolutevalue{j_1-j_2}\;.

(3.1.32)

For example,
\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}=1\oplus 0
indicates that we have both spin-1 and spin-0 operators constructed out of two fermions, i.e.
\psi_{\{\alpha}\psi_{\beta\}}
and
\psi_{[\alpha}\psi_{\beta]}=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}(\psi_{\sigma}\psi^{\sigma})
. In what follows, we will assume that all open
\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
indices are symmetrized as any antisymmetric pair can be rewritten in terms of contracted indices and
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}
. So, we say that
\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}=1\oplus 0
indicates that
\psi_{\alpha}\times\psi_{\beta}
can be decomposed into
\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}
and
\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}
.

In some cases, we are interested in CFTs with parity symmetry where the relevant algebra is enlarged from
\mathrm{so}(3)
to
\mathrm{o}(3)
which are labeled by the previous index
j
and a sign
p=\pm 1
. We will denote the representation as
j^{p}
, where
p
indicates if the corresponding irrep is parity even or parity odd. The decomposition of products of two irreps now becomes

\displaystyle j_{1}^{+}\otimes j_{2}^{+}=(j_{1}+j_{2})^{+}\oplus(j_{1}+j_{2}-1)^{-}\oplus(j_{1}+j_{2}-2)^{+}\oplus\cdots\oplus\absolutevalue{j_1-j_2}^{+|-}\;,

(3.1.33)

where parity alternates between different irreps. Likewise, if some of the parities are flipped on left hand side, so are those in the right hand side, e.g.
j_{1}^{+}\otimes j_{2}^{-}=(j_{1}+j_{2})^{-}\oplus\cdots
.

We now turn to the bispinors we can construct out of
\psi^{\alpha}
and
\psi^{\beta}
. The representation of the Majorana fermion can be taken as either
\frac{1}{2}^{+}
or
\frac{1}{2}^{-}
: this ambiguity reflects the fact that it is not the
\mathrm{O}(2,1)
but the
\mathrm{Pin}(2,1)
group which acts on the spinors. Nevertheless, as we will always be interested in composite operators or correlators with even number of fermions, our results will be ambiguity-free. Let us choose that
\psi_{\alpha}
transforms under the
\mathrm{Pin}(2,1)
with the representation
\frac{1}{2}^{+}
. We then have the decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes\frac{1}{2}^{+}=1^{+}\oplus 0^{-}\;,

(3.1.34)

which means we can construct a parity even vector and a parity odd scalar out of two
\psi
.
6
6

6

One can also check this explicitly. If we choose parity transformation as a reflection
x^{1}\rightarrow-x^{1}
and
x^{0,2}\rightarrow x^{0,2}
, the Majorana fermion transforms as
\psi^{\alpha}\rightarrow\pm(\gamma^{1}\psi)^{\alpha}
where the ambiguity is the same one discussed above. By using
\psi_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\psi^{\beta}
, we can show that
\psi_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mp(\psi\gamma^{1})_{\alpha}
, hence
\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}\rightarrow-(\psi\gamma^{1})_{\alpha}(\gamma^{1}\psi)^{\alpha}=-\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}
indicating that
\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}
is a parity-odd scalar. One can similarly check the parities of other operators.
These are exactly
\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}
and
\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}
, which should then appear in the double twist towers
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}]_{n}
and
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{n}
.

We can now include a partial derivative, which is parity even spin
-1
object. That means

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes 1^{+}=2^{+}\oplus 1_{2}^{-}\oplus 0^{+}\;,

(3.1.35)

where
a
denotes the multiplicity of the representation
j
in the notation
j_{a}
. These objects are

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma\lambda}\psi_{\beta}\;,\quad\psi_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta\gamma}\psi^{\alpha}\;,\quad\psi_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta\gamma}\psi^{\gamma}\;,\quad\psi_{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha}_{\;\;\beta}\psi^{\beta}\;.

(3.1.36)

First two objects already appear in
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}]_{n}
and
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{n}
double twist towers. We are left to conclude that inclusion of a derivative yields only two new towers: a parity odd tower
[\psi_{a}(\partial\psi)_{\beta}]_{n}
and a parity even tower
[\psi_{a}(\partial\psi)^{\alpha}]_{n}
.

Considering more derivatives will not produce new towers as we expect a total of four different double twist towers. To see that, we first note that out of the irreducibly representations in the decomposition of p-derivatives, i.e.
1^{+}\otimes 1^{+}\cdots\otimes 1^{+}
, only the fully symmetric representation is relevant as contracted derivatives can be rewritten in terms of
(\partial^{2})
and fewer number of derivatives with open indices due to the identity
\partial^{\alpha}_{\;\gamma}\partial^{\gamma}_{\;\beta}=(\partial^{2})\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
. Hence, the independent towers in the decomposition of two fermions with
p-
derivatives appear in
\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes p^{+}\in\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes 1^{+}\otimes 1^{+}\cdots\otimes 1^{+}
which is decomposed as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes\frac{1}{2}^{+}\otimes p^{+}=(p+1)^{+}\oplus p_{2}^{-}\oplus(p-1)^{+}\;,

(3.1.37)

which are the towers

\displaystyle{}[\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}]_{n}\;,\quad[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{n}\;,\quad[\psi_{\alpha}(\partial\psi)_{\beta}]_{n}\;,\quad[\psi_{\alpha}(\partial\psi)^{\alpha}]_{n}\;.

(3.1.38)

With the double twist towers constructed as above, we finally turn to the
selection rules
on spin imposed by the fact that double twist operators satisfy certain symmetries as they are defined modulo total derivatives. For example, via integration by parts we see that

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\dots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}\partial^{2n}\psi_{b}=(-1)^{\ell+1}\psi_{\beta}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\dots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}\partial^{2n}\psi_{\alpha}+\text{ total derivatives}\;,

(3.1.39)

hence
\psi_{(\alpha}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\dots\partial_{\mu_{p}}\partial^{2n}\psi_{b)}
is nonzero only if
p+1\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. This then indicates that the tower
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}]_{n}
has operators of even spin only. By a similar analysis, we show that only odd/even spin operators contribute to each double twist tower: we summarize them in Table. (
3.1
).

Table 3.1:
We list the different double-twist families for Majorana fermions in 3d, with their twist accumulation points, parities, and spins.

Family

Twist

Parity

Spin

[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{n,\ell}

2\Delta_{\psi}+2n-1

Even

\ell\geq 2
, Even

2\Delta_{\psi}+2n

Odd

\ell\geq 0
, Even

[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\;\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{n,\ell}

2\Delta_{\psi}+2n

Odd

, Odd
