3.2
Large spin expansion

3.2.1
Lightcone bootstrap

Short review

In the following calculations, we review the work of
[
126
]
which solves the lightcone bootstrap in a perturbative expansion. We start by considering the 4-point function of identical scalars,
\expectationvalue{\f\f\f\f}
. The 4-point function is invariant under
1\leftrightarrow 3
(or
s\leftrightarrow t
) crossing, which implies

\left(\frac{(1-z)(1-\bar{z})}{z\overline{z}}\right)^{2h_{\phi}}\sum_{\mathcal{O}}P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}g_{h_{\mathcal{O}},\overline{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}(z,\overline{z})=\sum_{\mathcal{O}}P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}g_{h_{\mathcal{O}},\overline{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-\overline{z},1-z)\;,

(3.2.1)

where
P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}=f_{\phi\phi\Omega}^{2}
.

We can now consider this equation in the lightcone limit
z\ll 1-\bar{z}\ll 1
. In the limit
z\ll 1
, the identity operator dominates on the left-hand side, while taking
1-\bar{z}\ll 1
allows us to use the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
expansion on the right-hand side. In this limit, the crossing equation becomes

\left(\frac{1-\bar{z}}{z}\right)^{2h_{\phi}}\approx\sum_{\mathcal{O}}P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}(1-\bar{z})^{h_{\mathcal{O}}}k_{2\overline{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-z)\;.

(3.2.2)

By the arguments of
[
111
,
112
]
, in order to match the
z\rightarrow 0
divergence on the left-hand side, which is not present in any individual
t
-channel block, we need to sum over operators with unbounded spin on the right-hand side. Specifically, we need a tower of “double-twist” operators in the
t
-channel,
[\phi\phi]_{0,\ell}
, such that
h_{0,\ell}\rightarrow 2h_{\phi}
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
.

At this point we could use a Bessel function approximation of the blocks to derive the large-
\ell
asymptotics of the OPE coefficients, but it will be more useful to use our knowledge of
1d
generalized free field theories to write down the exact sum
[
126
]

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{h}=-a+\ell\\
\ell=0,1,...\end{subarray}}S_{a}(\bar{h})k_{2\bar{h}}(1-z)=\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{a}\;,

(3.2.3a)

where

\displaystyle S_{a}(\bar{h})=\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h})^{2}\Gamma(\bar{h}-a-1)}{\Gamma(-a)^{2}\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+a+1)}\;.

(3.2.3b)

From (
3.2.3
), we now have at large
\ell
, where
\bar{h}\approx 2h_{\phi}+\ell
, the following result for the OPE coefficients:

P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}(\bar{h})\sim S_{-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h})\;.

(3.2.4)

The “
\sim
” is because with this approach we can only find the asymptotic expansion for the OPE coefficients at large
\bar{h}
. Note that by expanding to higher orders in
1-\bar{z}
one can also prove the existence of operators
[\phi\phi]_{n,\ell}
which have
h_{n,\ell}\rightarrow 2h_{\phi}+n
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
.

To extend these calculations to higher orders in the large-
\bar{h}
expansion, we can use the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
expansion on both sides of the crossing equation (
3.2.1
), expand in
z\ll 1-\bar{z}\ll 1
, and then use (
3.2.3
) to unambiguously match generic powers of
z
in the
s
-channel to the large-spin asymptotics of double-twist operators in the
t
-channel.

Of course, there are subtleties in this procedure which we have glossed over. First, for the arguments of
[
111
,
112
]
to work when matching a power of
z
in the
s
-channel to an infinite sum of
t
-channel blocks, we need the
s
-channel term to be more divergent than any individual
t
-channel block. From (
3.2.1
), we see this is only true if
h_{\mathcal{O}}<2h_{\phi}
. However, as noted in
[
115
]
, we can make any generic, individual power of
z^{a}
on the left-hand side of (
3.2.1
) as divergent as we like by repeatedly acting with a
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
Casimir differential operator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\equiv(1-z)^{2}z\partial_{z}^{2}+(1-z)^{2}\partial_{z}\;.

(3.2.5)

Since the
t
-channel
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
blocks are eigenfunctions of this Casimir, these differential operators leave the form of the
t
-channel expansion unchanged. Therefore, by acting with this differential operator sufficiently many times we can make the
s
-channel more divergent than the crossed channels.

In the terminology of
[
126
]
, generic powers
z^{a}
are “Casimir-singular”. On the other hand, terms like
z^{n}
and
z^{n}\log(z)
, with
n
a non-negative integer, are called “Casimir-regular”. If we repeatedly act with
\mathcal{C}
on these terms, we eventually get
0
. Therefore, we cannot use the arguments of
[
111
,
112
]
to match these terms with large-spin asymptotics of double-twist operators and they are more sensitive to finite-spin effects.

In the study of the lightcone bootstrap, there are a few places where Casimir-regular terms can appear. The first is if we start the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
sum (
3.2.3
) at a generic point
\bar{h}_{0}
:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{h}=\bar{h}_{0}+\ell\\
\ell=0,1,...\end{subarray}}S_{a}(\bar{h})k_{2\bar{h}}(1-z)=\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{a}+\mathcal{A}(\bar{h}_{0})\;,

(3.2.6)

where
\mathcal{A}(\bar{h}_{0})
is defined in
[
126
]
(we will not need its explicit form). Since the choice of starting point only affects a finite number of blocks, changing the lower limit of the sum will not affect predictions for large-spin asymptotics.

Another important issue is that our sums over blocks are not actually integer spaced. In general, the double-twist operators will get anomalous dimensions which also depend on the spin. Therefore, for a tower of double-twist operators
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
parametrized by the spin
\ell
we have

\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}}=2h_{\phi}+\ell+\delta h_{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}}\;,

(3.2.7)

where
\delta h_{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}}
is half the anomalous dimension with respect to the generalized free field value.

To account for this effect, we can reparametrize our sum by inserting a Jacobian:

\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}}}{\partial\ell}P(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}})k_{2\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}}}(1-z)=\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}P(2h_{\phi}+\ell)k_{4h_{\phi}+2\ell}(1-z)+\ldots\;,

(3.2.8)

where the dropped terms are Casimir-regular in
z
. In the current discussion we will not be concerned with matching Casimir-regular terms, but will rather focus on how we can use the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
expansion to match individual conformal blocks in the
s
-channel.

Thus, let us now consider the effect of single generic conformal block
g_{h_{i},\bar{h}_{i}}(z,\bar{z})
in the s-channel. In the limit
z\ll 1-\bar{z}\ll 1
it has the general form:

\displaystyle g_{h_{i},\bar{h}_{i}}(z,\bar{z})=\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{h_{i}}\left(A_{i}\log(1-\bar{z})+B_{i}+\Omega(1-\bar{z})\right)+\ldots\;,

(3.2.9)

where we have only written the leading order terms. It is straightforward to include
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
descendants using the results of
[
126
,
215
]
, and to expand to higher orders in
(1-\bar{z})
using the explicit form of the hypergeometric functions, where such terms are needed to fix the corrections for higher twist towers, i.e.
[\phi\phi]_{n>0}
.
7
7

7

We will drop the label
\ell
when referring to a given twist tower.
Here we will be primarily interested in the form of the correction for the leading twist
[\phi\phi]_{0}
operators.

The crossing equation then becomes

\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{-2h_{\phi}}+\sum_{i}\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{h_{i}-2h_{\phi}}(A_{i}\log(1-\bar{z})+B_{i}+\order{1-\bar{z}})\\
=\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}\in[\phi\phi]_{0}}P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}(1-\bar{z})^{h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}}k_{2\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-z)+\ldots\;.

(3.2.10)

To match the
\log
terms we have to expand in the anomalous dimension,
h_{\mathcal{O}}=2h_{\phi}+\delta h_{\mathcal{O}}
, and we find

\displaystyle P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}\sim{}

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}{\partial\ell}\left[S_{-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})+\sum_{i}B_{i}S_{h_{i}-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})\right]\;,

(3.2.11a)

\displaystyle\delta h_{\mathcal{O}}P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}\sim{}

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}{\partial\ell}\left[\sum_{i}A_{i}S_{h_{i}-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})\right]\;.

(3.2.11b)

The factors of
2
are because we only sum over double-twist operators of even spin in the
t
-channel. To find the asymptotic, large-spin behavior of the anomalous dimensions we then just need the ratio of these terms:

\delta h_{\mathcal{O}}\sim\frac{\sum_{i}A_{i}S_{h_{i}-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})}{S_{-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})+\sum_{i}B_{i}S_{h_{i}-2h_{\phi}}(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})}\;.

(3.2.12)

In order to compute
P_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}
we can then plug this into the relation
\frac{\partial\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}{\partial\ell}=\left(1-\frac{\partial\delta h_{\mathcal{O}}}{\partial\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}\right)^{-1}
.

Coefficient Expansions

In eqn. (
3.2.3b
), we provided the MFT coefficient
S_{a}(\overline{h})
for identical operators. Its generalization
S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})
are defined as

\displaystyle S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})\coloneqq\frac{1}{\Gamma(-a-r)\Gamma(-a-s)}\frac{\Gamma(\overline{h}-r)\Gamma(\overline{h}-s)}{\Gamma(2\overline{h}-1)}\frac{\Gamma(\overline{h}-a-1)}{\Gamma(\overline{h}+a+1)}\;,

(3.2.13)

which satisfy the one dimensional Mean Field Theory sum
[
126
]
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{h}=\ell-a\\
\ell=0,1,\dots\end{subarray}}S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})(1-z)^{\overline{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\hb-r,\hb+s}{2\hb};1-z\biggr]=\frac{z^{r+a}}{(1-z)^{a}}\;.

(3.2.14)

S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})
scales like
4^{-\overline{h}}\overline{h}^{-\frac{3}{2}-2a-r-s}
at large
\overline{h}
, meaning that we can expand
S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})
as

\displaystyle S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k,a}^{r,s,m,n}S_{a-\frac{m+n-k}{2}}^{r+m,s+n}(\overline{h})\;,

(3.2.15)

with
\overline{h}
-independent coefficients
c_{k,a}^{r,s,m,n}
. Since we are working at next-to-next-to-leading order in
1/\overline{h}
, we can truncate this expansion as

\displaystyle S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})\approxeq c_{0,a}^{r,s,m,n}S_{a-\frac{m+n}{2}}^{r+m,s+n}(\overline{h})+c_{1,a}^{r,s,m,n}S_{a-\frac{m+n-1}{2}}^{r+m,s+n}(\overline{h})+c_{2,a}^{r,s,m,n}S_{a-\frac{m+n-2}{2}}^{r+m,s+n}(\overline{h})\;.

(3.2.16)

Armed with this, let us consider the following summation which appears repeatedly in eqn. (
3.2.36
) after insertion of the ansatz in eqn. (
3.2.38
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\overline{h}}{\partial\ell}S_{a}^{0,0}(\overline{h})(1-z)^{\overline{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\hb-m,\hb+n}{2\hb};1-z\biggr]\;.

(3.2.17)

For parity-even structures,
m,n=0
, hence we can immediately use eqn. (
3.2.37
). For parity-odd structures, we can expand
S^{0,0}
in terms of
S^{m,n}
and obtain

\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\overline{h}}{\partial\ell}S_{a}^{0,0}(\overline{h})(1-z)^{\overline{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\hb-m,\hb+n}{2\hb};1-z\biggr]\\
=z^{a+\frac{m-n}{2}}\left(c_{0,a}^{r,s}+c_{1,a}^{r,s}{\sqrt{z}}+\left(\frac{2a-m-n}{2}c_{0,a}^{r,s}+c_{2,a}^{r,s}\right)z+\order{z^{3/2}}\right)\;,

(3.2.18)

where
c_{k,a}^{m,n}\equiv c_{k,a}^{0,0,m,n}
. This equation simply means that we replace factors of
\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{a}
in eqn. (
3.2.39
) with the expression above for parity-odd structures.

We list below the coefficients
c_{0}
,
c_{1}
, and
c_{2}
for the reader’s convenience:

\displaystyle c_{k,a}^{r,s,m,n}=\kappa_{k}\frac{\Gamma\left(-a-\frac{m}{2}+\frac{n}{2}-r-\frac{k}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(-a+\frac{m}{2}-\frac{n}{2}-s-\frac{k}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(-a-r)\Gamma(-a-s)}\;,\quad k\in\{0,1,2\}\;,

(3.2.19)

with

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}=

\displaystyle 1\;,

(3.2.20a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=

\displaystyle-\frac{m(m+2r)+n(n+2s)}{2}\;,

(3.2.20b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}m^{4}+\frac{4r-1}{8}m^{3}+\frac{n(1+2n+4s)+4r(r-1)-2(a+1)}{8}m^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{n^{2}(1+4r)+n(8rs-4)-4a(n-2)+4(a^{2}-r^{2}+1)}{8}m

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{8}\left((n+2s)^{2}(n-1)-2n-2a(n-4)+4(a^{2}+1)\right)\;.

(3.2.20c)

3.2.2
Fermion conformal blocks and the crossing equations

Having reviewed lightcone bootstrap for scalars, we will now move on to 4-point functions of fermions
\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\psi\psi}
in 3d. Our focus for now will be to compute the first several perturbative
1/\bar{h}
corrections to the
[\psi\psi]_{0}
coefficients and anomalous dimensions, generalizing the method of section
3.2.1
to fermions. As illustrated in
[
126
]
, such perturbative calculations are in many cases numerically sufficient and also quite useful in providing consistency checks on formulas obtained from the inversion formula approach.

We will be using the method of
[
81
]
to generate the fermion conformal blocks by hitting the four-scalar conformal block with differential operators in the embedding space.
8
8

8

A review of the embedding space formalism can be found in section
3.1.1
.
Specifically, a contribution to
\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\psi\psi}
takes the form:

\left(\frac{X_{24}}{X_{14}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}}\left(\frac{X_{14}}{X_{13}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}\frac{t_{I}g^{I;a,b}_{h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})}{X_{12}^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+1}{2}}X_{34}^{\frac{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}+1}{2}}}\\
=\mathrm{D}_{a}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{b}\left[\left(\frac{X_{24}}{X_{14}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}}\left(\frac{X_{14}}{X_{13}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}\frac{g_{h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})}{X_{12}^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2}}X_{34}^{\frac{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}{2}}}\right]\;,

(3.2.21)

where
t_{I}
are different 4-point structures in embedding space,
a,b
are indices denoting possible 3-point structures, and we have suppressed the dependence of the blocks on the external dimensions. The operators
\mathrm{D}_{a}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{1}=\expectationvalue{S_1S_2}\Pi_{1^{+}2^{+}}\;,

(3.2.22a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{2}=-\expectationvalue{S_1\frac{\delta}{\delta X_1}\frac{\delta}{\delta X_2}S_2}\Pi_{1^{-}2^{-}}\;,

(3.2.22b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{3}=\expectationvalue{S_1\frac{\delta}{\delta X_1}S_2}\Pi_{1^{-}2^{+}}-\expectationvalue{S_2\frac{\delta}{\delta X_2}S_1}\Pi_{1^{+}2^{-}}\;,

(3.2.22c)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{4}=\expectationvalue{S_1\frac{\delta}{\delta X_1}S_2}\Pi_{1^{-}2^{+}}+\expectationvalue{S_2\frac{\delta}{\delta X_2}S_1}\Pi_{1^{+}2^{-}}\;,

(3.2.22d)

where
\Pi_{a^{i}b^{j}}
are operators which shift external dimensions such that

\displaystyle\Pi_{a^{i}b^{j}}:\{\Delta_{a},\Delta_{b}\}\rightarrow\{\Delta_{a}+\frac{i}{2},\Delta_{b}+\frac{j}{2}\}\;.

(3.2.23)

The corresponding operators acting on the points
(X_{3},X_{4})
are found via the replacement

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{a}\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{a}\evaluated{}_{(1,2)\rightarrow(3,4)}\;.

(3.2.24)

An alternative basis denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{i}
was used in
[
81
]
, given by:
9
9

9

There is also an additional term in
\mathcal{D}_{4}
which vanishes for identical external operators.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}=

\displaystyle\;\mathrm{D}_{1}\;,

(3.2.25a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{4(\overline{h}-h)(\overline{h}+h-1)}\left(\mathrm{D}_{2}-(2h+2\Delta_{\psi}-4)(2h-2\Delta_{\psi}+1)\mathrm{D}_{1}\right)\;,

(3.2.25b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{3}=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{2(\overline{h}+h-1)}\mathrm{D}_{3}\;,

(3.2.25c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{4}=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{2(\overline{h}-h)}\mathrm{D}_{4}\;.

(3.2.25d)

These two bases have different merits. One nice feature of the
\mathrm{D}_{i}
basis is that the operators are independent of
(h,\bar{h})
, so there is a clean separation between the calculation of double-twist data and the differential operators, i.e. we do not want these operators to also depend on the anomalous dimensions. By comparison
\mathcal{D}_{i}
generates the most natural basis of embedding space, 3-point tensor structures. We find that it is most convenient to use the
\mathrm{D}_{i}
basis when performing the calculations and presenting the results.

The operators
\mathrm{D}_{1}
and
\mathrm{D}_{2}
generate parity-even structures, whereas
\mathrm{D}_{3}
and
\mathrm{D}_{4}
generate parity-odd ones. When the external fermions are identical, there are also selection rules on the spins of the exchanged operators:
\mathrm{D}_{1}
,
\mathrm{D}_{2}
, and
\mathrm{D}_{3}
are associated to operators of even spin while odd spins are associated with
\mathrm{D}_{4}
.

Now let us write down the condition from crossing symmetry. For convenience we will define the prefactor
\mathsf{p}
as

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}\left(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{X_{24}}{X_{14}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}}\left(\frac{X_{14}}{X_{13}}\right)^{\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}X_{12}^{-\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2}}X_{34}^{-\frac{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}{2}}\;,

(3.2.26)

and introduce the shorthand notations

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}

\displaystyle\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}\left(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\right)\;,

(3.2.27a)

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{\psi}

\displaystyle\equiv{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2},\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2},\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2},\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\;.

(3.2.27b)

Then we can write a more compact form of the conformal block for identical fermions:

\displaystyle t_{I}g^{I;a,b}_{h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})=\frac{\left(\mathrm{D}_{a}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{b}\left[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}\;g_{h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})\right]\right)_{\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{\psi}}}{{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{\psi}}\;,

(3.2.28)

and crossing symmetry implies

\displaystyle{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{\psi}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}t_{I}P_{\mathcal{O}}^{a,b}g^{I;ab}_{h,\overline{h}}(z,\overline{z})=-\left({\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{\psi}t_{I}\right)\evaluated{}_{1\leftrightarrow 3}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}P_{\mathcal{O}}^{a,b}g^{I;ab}_{h,\overline{h}}(1-\overline{z},1-z)\;,

(3.2.29)

where we use
P
for the coefficients in the
\mathrm{D}_{a}
differential basis.

To simplify some expressions, we will first consider the contributions of double-twist operators in the
(12)\rightarrow(34)
OPE. Then later we will take
1\leftrightarrow 3
so that they appear in the
t
-channel, and match their sum to the contributions of individual
s
-channel blocks, as in section
3.2.1
.

The 3-point structures will be unimportant in the following discussion so we will simplify the notation of the left-hand side of (
3.2.29
) to

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{different}\\
\text{ structures}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}\left[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}P_{\mathcal{O}}g_{h,\overline{h}}(z,\overline{z})\right]\right)\evaluated{}_{\Delta_i\rightarrow\Delta_\psi}\;.

(3.2.30)

We can also interchange the order of differential operators and summation over relevant operator families. Since
{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}
is independent of the exchanged operator, the double-twist sum reduces to

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{different}\\
\text{ structures}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}\left[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}P_{\mathcal{O}}g_{h,\overline{h}}(z,\overline{z})\right]\right)_{\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{\psi}}\;.

(3.2.31)

To go any further, we need to specify which operators
\mathcal{O}
must appear to reproduce the lightcone limit of the crossed channel. In particular, we know that an infinite sum of double-twist operators is needed to reproduce the identity operator in the crossed channel
[
126
,
112
,
111
]
. The required operators and their quantum numbers are schematically shown in Table
3.1
.

To remove clutter, we will denote different double-twist families generically as
[\psi\psi]_{n}
below. Their contribution then reads as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{different}\\
\text{ structures}\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}\bigg[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}P_{[\psi\psi]_{n}}g_{h_{[\psi\psi]_{n}},\,\bar{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{n}}}(z,\overline{z})\bigg]\Bigg)_{\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{\psi}}\;,

(3.2.32)

where the summation over all relevant families
[\psi\psi]_{n}
appearing in Table
3.1
is implicit.

Restoring possible dependence on the external dimension differences
r,s
(which arise from the shift operators
\Pi
), we will now use Eq. (
2.3.7
) in a slightly modified form after applying
g(z,\overline{z})=g(\overline{z},z)
symmetry:

\displaystyle g_{h,\overline{h}}^{r,s}(z,\overline{z})=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{n}A_{n,j}^{r,s}(h,\overline{h})\bar{z}^{h+n}k^{r,s}_{2(\bar{h}+j)}(z)\;.

(3.2.33)

By expanding

\displaystyle h_{[\psi\psi]_{n}}=h_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}+n+\delta h_{[\psi\psi]_{n}}\;,

(3.2.34)

the leading part of the double-twist sum at small
\bar{z}
can then be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{different}\\
\text{ structures}\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}\bigg[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}P_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}\overline{z}^{h_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}+\delta h_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}k^{r,s}_{2\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}(z)\bigg]\Bigg)_{\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{\psi}}\left(1+\order{{\zb}}\right)\;.

(3.2.35)

To reproduce
\log
terms in the crossed channel, we will expand to linear order in the anomalous dimension:
10
10

10

Higher order terms in
\delta h
are matched with multi-twist operators in the
s
-channel.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{different}\\
\text{ structures}\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}\bigg[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}\;\overline{z}^{h_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}P_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}k^{r,s}_{2\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}(z)+\log(\zb)\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}P_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}\delta h_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}k^{r,s}_{2\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}(z)\bigg\}\bigg]\Bigg)_{\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{\psi}}.

(3.2.36)

In
[
126
]
it was shown how to sum over
{}_{2}F_{1}
hypergeometric functions to reproduce terms Casimir-singular in
z
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\overline{h}}{\partial\ell}S_{a}^{r,s}(\overline{h})k^{r,s}_{2\overline{h}}(1-z)=\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{a}+[\cdots]_{z}\;,

(3.2.37)

which is the generalization of eqn. (
3.2.3
) to non-identical external scaling dimensions. The explicit form of
S_{a}^{r,s}(h)
is given in eqn. (
3.2.13
), though it will not be necessary for the following calculations.

As explained in section
3.2.1
, we make the ansatz

\displaystyle P_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}^{a,b}(\bar{h})=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}{\partial\ell}\right)\sum\limits_{\{i\}}A_{a,b,i}S_{i}^{0,0}(\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}})\;,

(3.2.38a)

\displaystyle(\delta hP)^{a,b}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}(\bar{h})=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}{\partial\ell}\right)\sum\limits_{\{j\}}B_{a,b,j}S_{j}^{0,0}(\overline{h}_{[\psi\psi]_{0}})\;,

(3.2.38b)

and insert this into eqn. (
3.2.36
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{different}\\
\text{ structures}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathrm{D}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}\left[{\rm Phys.\penalty\ Rev.\penalty\ E}_{i}\overline{z}^{h_{[\psi\psi]_{0}}}\left(\sum\limits_{\{i\}}A_{a,b,i}\left(\frac{1-z}{z}\right)^{i}+\log(\zb)\sum\limits_{\{j\}}B_{a,b,j}\left(\frac{1-z}{z}\right)^{j}\right)\right]\right)_{\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{\psi}}\;.

(3.2.39)

The
1/2
in front accounts for the fact that we are summing over operator families with even-integer spacing
[
126
]
, since we are dealing with identical external fermions. In particular,
P^{4,4}=0
for even
\ell
, and
P^{1,1}=P^{1,2}=P^{2,1}=P^{2,2}=P^{3,3}=0
for odd
\ell

[
81
]
.

After acting with the differential operators we take
1\leftrightarrow 3
and
(z,\bar{z})\rightarrow(1-z,1-\bar{z})
and match to individual
s
-channel blocks. We will find the sets
\{i\}
and
\{j\}
, and the coefficients
A
and
B
by matching the
s
-channel.

There is actually a subtle point we skipped while going from eqn. (
3.2.37
) to eqn. (
3.2.39
). As evident from eqn. (
3.2.22
), we need
S^{\pm\frac{1}{2},\pm\frac{1}{2}}
to be able to use eqn. (
3.2.37
) for parity-odd structures even though we used
S^{0,0}
in our ansatz above. We resolve this in appendix
3.2.1
by expanding
S_{a}^{r+n,r+m}
in terms of
S_{a+k}^{r,s}
.

3.2.3
Results

In this section, we first discuss identity matching and find the MFT solutions. Then we consider the exchange of parity-even and parity-odd operators of arbitrary dimension and spin, and calculate their contribution to the OPE coefficients and anomalous dimensions of the double-twist families
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
,
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0,\ell}
, and
[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\;\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
at leading and sub-leading order in the small
z
expansion. As special cases, we will present the contributions due to stress tensor exchange and scalar exchanges.

The reader is reminded that the contributions of all double-twist families are present, but we simply match the subset of terms relevant for the above families. For example, we match the
\order{z^{1-\Delta_\psi}(1-\bar{z})^0}
contribution of the stress tensor without matching the
\order{z^{-\Delta_\psi}(1-\bar{z})^1}
contribution from identity exchange, even though the latter is more dominant in the lightcone limit. However, these contributions come from different twist families in the crossed channel, and there is no mixing for the terms leading order in
(1-\bar{z})
. For instance, both
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
and
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{1,\ell}
bring contributions of order
\order{z^{-\Delta_\psi}(1-\bar{z})^1}
, however only
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
brings
\order{z^{-\Delta_\psi}(1-\bar{z})^0}
terms. So by requiring
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
to reproduce these in the crossed channel, we can extract its OPE coefficients and anomalous dimensions.

Below, we will suppress the label for the double twist families whenever there is no ambiguity. For example, we will simply write the OPE coefficient
f^{1}_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}}
as
f^{1}
. We can extract the relevant family due to the conditions listed in Table
3.1
and the fact that the four types of 3-point functions
f^{a}
are associated with (parity, spin) as: (
+
, even), (
+
, even), (
-
, even), and (
-
, odd), respectively.

Identity matching

Let us first focus on the identity contribution alone. In the
s
-channel the relevant terms are
\order{z^{-\frac{1}{2}-\Delta_\psi}(1-\bar{z})^0}
and
\order{z^{\frac{1}{2}-\Delta_\psi}(1-\bar{z})^0}
. We reproduce these terms in the
t
-channel by tuning
A_{a,b,i}
in eqn. (
3.2.38a
); for example, we need

\displaystyle\left\{A_{2,2,\frac{3}{2}-\Delta_{\psi}},A_{2,2,\frac{5}{2}-\Delta_{\psi}}\right\}

(3.2.40)

for the double-twist family
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
. The fact that
A_{1,1,i}
,
A_{1,2,i}
, and
A_{2,1,i}
are zero reflects the vanishing of the 3-point coefficient
f^{1}
at all orders.

As there is no anomalous dimension for the identity exchange alone we have
\frac{\partial\overline{h}}{\partial\ell}=1
, hence we can immediately get
P^{a,b}
with eqn. (
3.2.38
), then solve for the physical 3-point coefficients
11
11

11

The
(-1)^{\ell}
term here would be absent in the notation of
[
81
]
, however we need it as our conformal block normalization in eqn. (
2.3.6
) differs from that paper by a factor of
(-1)^{\ell}
.

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{O}}^{a,b}=(-1)^{\ell}f_{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\mathcal{O}}^{a}f_{\psi_{3}\psi_{4}\mathcal{O}}^{b}\;,

(3.2.41)

where the identity itself has the OPE coefficients
f^{1}_{\psi\psi\mathbb{1}}=i
and
f^{2}_{\psi\psi\mathbb{1}}=0
.

Using the steps described above, we compute the OPE coefficients

\displaystyle f^{1}=\,

\displaystyle 0\;,

(3.2.42a)

\displaystyle f^{2}=\,

\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}}{4\overline{h}^{2}}\left(1-\frac{8\Delta_{\psi}-17}{16\bar{h}}-\frac{256\Delta_{\psi}^{3}-2112\Delta_{\psi}^{2}+4208\Delta_{\psi}-2787}{1536\overline{h}^{2}}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{3}\right)\;,

(3.2.42b)

\displaystyle f^{3}=\,

\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}}{2\overline{h}}\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{\psi}-1}{2\overline{h}}}\left(1-\frac{24\Delta_{\psi}-31}{16\bar{h}}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\right)\;,

(3.2.42c)

\displaystyle f^{4}=\,

\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}}{2\overline{h}}\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{\psi}-1}{2\overline{h}}}\left(1+\frac{8\Delta_{\psi}-1}{16\bar{h}}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\right)\;,

(3.2.42d)

where for convenience we have defined the prefactor
f_{0}
as

\displaystyle f_{0}\equiv i\sqrt[4]{\pi}\frac{2^{\frac{3}{2}-\bar{h}}\bar{h}^{\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{4}}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\;.

(3.2.43)

Note that the results take a slightly simpler form in the
\mathcal{D}
basis. E.g., at leading order

\displaystyle f^{2}\evaluated{}_{\cD}=f_{0}\;,\qquad f^{3,4}\evaluated{}_{\cD}=f_{0}\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{\psi}-1}{2\overline{h}}}\;,

(3.2.44)

which follows from eqn. (
3.2.25
).

Matching the exchange of a generic parity-even operator

Let us turn to the contribution of the exchange of a generic parity-even operator
\mathcal{O}^{+}_{\tau,\ell}
of twist
\tau
and spin
\ell
in the
s
-channel to the double-twist families
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}
,
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}
, and
[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}
in the
t
-channel.

When calculating corrections to the anomalous dimensions of double-twist families we need to recall that the contributions of multiple operators are not additive. Additionally, in general there can be multiple double-twist families that mix with each other. The full formula for their anomalous dimension matrix is
12
12

12

The anomalous dimension matrix (
3.2.45
) is diagonal if there is no mixing between the double-twist families.

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{[\psi\psi]}}{2}=\delta h_{[\psi\psi]}=\frac{\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\delta h_{[\psi\psi]}P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}J^{-1}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathcal{O}}}{\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}\left(P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}J^{-1}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathcal{O}}}\;,

(3.2.45)

where
J
is the Jacobian

\displaystyle J_{[\psi\psi]}\equiv\frac{\partial\delta h_{[\psi\psi]}}{\partial\overline{h}}\;.

(3.2.46)

Here
\mathcal{O}
runs over all exchanged operators in the
s
-channel. Likewise, the OPE coefficients
f^{a}_{[\psi\psi]}
are given as

\displaystyle(-1)^{\ell}f_{[\psi\psi]}^{a}f_{[\psi\psi]}^{b}=J_{[\psi\psi]}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}\left(P^{a,b}_{[\psi\psi]}J^{-1}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathcal{O}}\;.

(3.2.47)

In the large
\bar{h}
expansion, we can of course truncate the summation over operators in twist to extract the large
\bar{h}
behavior. For example, in the first few orders, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{[\psi\psi]}}{2}=\;\delta h_{[\psi\psi]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\delta h_{[\psi\psi]}P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}\right)_{\mathbbm{O}}}{\left(P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}\right)_{\mathbb{1}}+\left(P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}\right)_{\mathbbm{O}}}\;,

(3.2.48a)

\displaystyle(-1)^{\ell}f_{[\psi\psi]}^{a}f_{[\psi\psi]}^{b}

\displaystyle=\left(P^{a,b}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathbb{1}}+\left(P^{a,b}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathbbm{O}}\;,

(3.2.48b)

for the identity operator
\mathbb{1}
along with the operator with minimum twist
\mathbbm{O}
, which is usually either the stress tensor or a scalar of low dimension.

At leading order in
1/\bar{h}
one can easily isolate the contribution of any operator. Only the identity operator contributes in the denominator in eqn. (
3.2.45
), allowing one to write an isolated contribution to the anomalous dimension. Likewise, at leading order, one can immediately calculate an individual contribution to
f^{a}
.

Once we go beyond leading order, we can work with
\left(P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}J^{-1}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\left(\delta h^{a,b}_{[\psi\psi]}P_{[\psi\psi]}^{a,b}J^{-1}_{[\psi\psi]}\right)_{\mathcal{O}}
, make an ansatz for their large
\bar{h}
behavior, and then calculate the corrections to the 3-point coefficients and anomalous dimensions. We find

\displaystyle\left(P^{1,1}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{f_{+}^{2}}{16\bar{h}^{4}(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1})^{2}(H_{\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}-1})^{2}}

(3.2.49a)

\displaystyle\times\left[\left\{\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}\right)^{2}(5-4\Delta_{\psi})-4\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\right)^{2}\ell(\ell+\tau-1)(-2\Delta_{\psi}+\tau+1)^{2}\right\}^{2}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}\right]\;,

\displaystyle\hskip-144.54pt\left(P^{2,2}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{f_{+}^{2}\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}\right)^{2}}{16\bar{h}^{4}}\Bigg[1+\frac{-8\Delta_{\psi}+4\tau+17}{8\bar{h}}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg]\;,

(3.2.49b)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.05942pt\left((\delta hP)^{2,2}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{f_{+}^{2}}{32\overline{h}^{4}H_{\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}-1}}\Bigg[\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}\right)^{2}\left(1+\frac{17+4\tau-8\Delta_{\psi}}{8\overline{h}}\right)

(3.2.49c)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(2\ell-1)(2\ell+2\tau-1)\bar{h}^{2}}\Bigg(\frac{\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}\right)^{2}}{384}-f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\ell(\tau-1)(\ell+\tau-1)\left(-2\Delta_{\psi}+\tau+1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\right)^{2}\ell(\ell+\tau-1)\left(4\ell\tau+4(\ell-1)\ell+2\tau^{2}-5\tau+2\right)\left(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1\right)^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{4\ell^{2}\left(-8\tau^{3}+72\tau^{2}+512\tau+1851\right)+16\tau^{4}-128\tau^{3}-928\tau^{2}-3214\tau

\displaystyle+1827-4\ell\left(8\tau^{4}-80\tau^{3}-440\tau^{2}-1339\tau+1851\right)-128(2\ell-1)\Delta_{\psi}^{3}(2\ell+2\tau-1)

\displaystyle-16\Delta_{\psi}\left(\ell^{2}(96\tau+652)+\ell\left(96\tau^{2}+556\tau-652\right)-42\tau^{2}-302\tau+157\right)

\displaystyle+192\Delta_{\psi}^{2}\left(2\ell^{2}(\tau+13)+2\ell\left(\tau^{2}+12\tau-13\right)-\tau^{2}-12\tau+6\right)\bigg\}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{3}\Bigg]\;,

\displaystyle\left(P^{3,3}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}=

\displaystyle-\left(P^{4,4}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}

(3.2.49d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{f_{+}^{2}f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}}{16\overline{h}^{3}}\Bigg[\left(1+\frac{-8\Delta_{\psi}+4\tau+15}{8\bar{h}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}(\tau+2)-2\Delta_{\psi}(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}-4f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\ell(\ell+\tau-1))-4f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\ell(\tau+1)(\ell+\tau-1)\bigg\}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg]\;,

\displaystyle\left((\delta hP)^{3,3}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{f_{+}^{2}}{32\bar{h}^{3}H_{\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}-1}}\Bigg[f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-2)-4f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\ell(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1)(\ell+\tau-1)\right)

(3.2.49e)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\bar{h}}\Bigg((f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1})^{2}\left(48\Delta_{\psi}^{2}+\tau(4\tau+35)-2\Delta_{\psi}(16\tau+55)+62\right)

\displaystyle-4f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\ell(24\Delta_{\psi}-4\tau-23)(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1)(\ell+\tau-1)

\displaystyle+16(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2})^{2}\ell(\tau-2)(\ell+\tau-1)(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1)^{2}\Bigg)+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg]\;,

\displaystyle\left((\delta hP)^{4,4}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}=

\displaystyle-\left((\delta hP)^{3,3}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}+\frac{f_{+}^{2}}{32\bar{h}^{4}H_{\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}-1}}\Bigg[(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1})^{2}(4(\Delta_{\psi}-1)(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-2)-\tau)

(3.2.49f)

\displaystyle-8f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\ell(2\Delta_{\psi}-1)(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1)(\ell+\tau-1)

\displaystyle+4(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2})^{2}\ell(\tau-2)(\ell+\tau-1)(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1)^{2}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}\Bigg]\;,

where
H_{a}
is the Harmonic number, and we defined

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 1}\equiv f_{\mathcal{O}}^{1}+\left(2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau-1\right)\left(2\Delta_{\psi}+\tau-4\right)f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}

(3.2.50)

and

\displaystyle f_{+}\equiv\frac{2^{\frac{3}{2}-\bar{h}+\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}}\bar{h}^{\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{4}(1+2\tau)}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1-\tau}{2}\right)}\sqrt{\left(\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}\right)_{\frac{1}{2}}H_{\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}-1}}

(3.2.51)

for convenience. Note that
f_{+}
reduces back to eqn. (
3.2.43
) for identity exchange after setting
\ell=\tau=\delta h=0
. The appearance of
(f^{\prime 1}_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}
in the denominator of
P^{1,1}
is because we compute it by first matching
P^{1,2}
and then using the relation
P^{1,1}=(P^{1,2})^{2}/P^{2,2}
.

Reproducing identity matching:

As a consistency check, let us use this general form to reproduce the identity exchange contribution to the
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}
family. We can first check that the anomalous dimension due to sole identity exchange is indeed zero:
13
13

13

One sees a possible divergence if one does not take
f_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}\rightarrow 0
first. This is natural because the corresponding 3-point structure does not exist for scalars so its coefficient should be taken to vanish before setting
\ell=0
.

\displaystyle\gamma_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}}=2\delta h_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0,\ell}}=2\frac{\left((\delta hP)^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}}{\left(P^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow i,\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\;\tau\rightarrow 0}}=0\;.

(3.2.52)

With this, one can now straightforwardly calculate

\displaystyle\left(f^{1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(P^{1,1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow i,\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\;\tau\rightarrow 0}}\;,

(3.2.53a)

\displaystyle\left(f^{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(P^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow i,\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\;\tau\rightarrow 0}}\;,

(3.2.53b)

which match eqn. (
3.2.42
).

Parity-even scalar exchange:

We can also consider the special case of exchange of a parity-even scalar of twist
\tau_{s}
. Let us write down the anomalous dimension to leading order in
1/\overline{h}
for convenience. We then only need to use
P^{i,j}
for identity exchange and
(\delta hP)^{i,j}
for scalar exchange.

We can immediately read the leading-order anomalous dimensions due to a parity-even scalar exchange as follows:

\gamma_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}=2\frac{\left((\delta hP)^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow f_\phi,\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\;\tau\rightarrow\tau_s}}}{\left(P^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow f_\mathbb{1},\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\;\tau\rightarrow 0}}}=\frac{f^{2}_{\phi}}{\overline{h}^{\tau_{s}}}\frac{2^{{\tau_{s}}}\left(\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}\right)_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(-\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)_{\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\;,

(3.2.54a)

and likewise

\gamma_{[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}}=\gamma_{[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}=\frac{f^{2}_{\phi}}{\overline{h}^{\tau_{s}}}\frac{2^{{\tau_{s}}}\left(\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}\right)_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(-\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)_{\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}}\right)^{2}\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1-\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)}\;.

(3.2.54b)

There are two comments in order. Firstly,
\gamma_{[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}}
and
\gamma_{[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}
naïvely seem to be divergent in the MFT limit
\Delta_{\psi}\rightarrow 1
. However, what really matters when solving the analytic bootstrap is the weighted contribution
(f_{{[\psi\psi]}_{0}})^{2}\gamma_{{[\psi\psi]}_{0}}
. We can check from eqn. (
3.2.42
) that the squared OPE coefficients also vanish linearly as
\Delta_{\psi}\rightarrow 1
. So, we need to check that this weighted contribution in fact vanishes in mean field theory, or that the factor

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)_{\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(-\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2})}

(3.2.55)

is zero. In mean field theory we have the contributions from scalar operators
[\psi_{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha\beta}\psi_{\beta}]_{n}
with twist
2\Delta_{\psi}+1+2n
for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Plugging in the mean field theory twist we obtain a factor of
\Gamma(-n)^{-1}
, so the result vanishes as expected.

Secondly, we recall that
f_{\phi}\equiv f_{\psi\psi\phi}
is purely imaginary due to the Grassmann nature of fermions, hence
\gamma_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}<0
, as expected for the leading double-twist trajectory
[
112
]
.

Stress tensor exchange:

As a last example we consider stress tensor exchange. From Ward identities, we know that
14
14

14

See
[
81
]
for their calculation in the
\mathcal{D}
basis.

\displaystyle f_{\psi\psi T}^{1}=\frac{3i\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)\left(2\Delta_{\psi}+1\right)}{16\sqrt{C_{T}}}\;,\quad f_{\psi\psi T}^{2}=-\frac{3i}{32\sqrt{C_{T}}}\;,

(3.2.56)

where
C_{T}
is the central charge. Hence, we compute

\gamma_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}=2\frac{\left((\delta hP)^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow f_{\psi\psi T}^1,\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow f_{\psi\psi T}^2,\;l\rightarrow 2,\;\tau\rightarrow 1}}}{\left(P^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow f_\mathbb{1},\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\;\tau\rightarrow 0}}}=-\frac{48\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}{\pi C_{T}\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)^{2}\overline{h}}\;,

(3.2.57a)

and

\gamma_{[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}}=\gamma_{[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}=-\frac{24\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)\left(2\Delta_{\psi}+1\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}{\pi C_{T}\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}\right)^{2}\overline{h}}\;.

(3.2.57b)

Matching the exchange of a generic parity-odd operator

Similar to the previous computation, we can also consider the contribution of the exchange of a generic parity-odd operator
\mathcal{O}^{-}_{\tau,\ell}
in the
s
-channel to the double-twist operators
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}
,
[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}
, and
[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}
in the
t
-channel.

The analogue of eqn. (
3.2.49
) now reads as

\displaystyle\left(P^{1,2}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{-}}=-f_{-}^{2}\Bigg[\left(\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\right)^{2}-\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\right)^{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{-8\Delta_{\psi}+4\tau+13}{8\bar{h}}\right)+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg]\;,

(3.2.58a)

\left((\delta hP)^{2,2}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}^{-}_{\tau,\ell}}=-\frac{f_{-}^{2}}{8\overline{h}^{2}}\Bigg[\frac{(2\ell+\tau-1)\left(\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\right)^{2}\ell-\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\right)^{2}(\tau+\ell-1)\right)}{\ell(\ell+\tau-1)}\\
\times\left(1+\frac{-8\Delta_{\psi}+4\tau+21}{8\bar{h}}\right)+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg],

(3.2.58b)

\displaystyle\left((\delta hP)^{3,3}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}^{-}_{\tau,\ell}}=-\frac{f_{-}^{2}}{4}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\left(\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\right)^{2}+\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\right)^{2}\right)(2\ell+\tau-1)+\frac{2\ell+\tau-1}{8\overline{h}}

(3.2.58c)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\frac{\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\right)^{2}\left(8(1-3\ell)\Delta_{\psi}+4(\ell-1)\tau+23\ell-4\right)}{\ell}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\right)^{2}\left(-8\Delta_{\psi}(3\ell+3\tau-2)+(\ell+\tau)(4\tau+23)-19\right)}{\ell+\tau-1}\bigg\}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg],

\displaystyle\left((\delta hP)^{4,4}J^{-1}\right)_{\mathcal{O}^{-}_{\tau,\ell}}=-\frac{f_{-}^{2}}{4}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\left(\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\right)^{2}+\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\right)^{2}\right)(2\ell+\tau-1)+\frac{2\ell+\tau-1}{8\overline{h}}

(3.2.58d)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\frac{\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\right)^{2}\left(8(\ell-1)\Delta_{\psi}+4(\ell+1)\tau+7\ell+4\right)}{\ell}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\right)^{2}\left(8\Delta_{\psi}(\ell+\tau)+\ell(4\tau+7)+\tau(4\tau-1)-11\right)}{\ell+\tau-1}\bigg\}+\order{\frac{1}{\hb}}^{2}\Bigg],

where for convenience we have defined

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}\equiv{}

\displaystyle 2(\tau+\ell-1)f_{\mathcal{O}}^{3}\;,

(3.2.59a)

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}\equiv{}

\displaystyle 2\ell f_{\mathcal{O}}^{4}\;,

(3.2.59b)

\displaystyle f_{-}\equiv{}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{\frac{\tau}{2}-\bar{h}+\ell-1}\bar{h}^{\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{4}(7+2\tau)}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{\tau}{2}\right)\sqrt{\left(\ell+\frac{\tau}{2}\right)_{\frac{1}{2}}}}\;.

(3.2.59c)

Note that
f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 3}
and
f_{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime 4}
are actually the 3-point coefficients in the
\mathcal{D}
basis as one can see by comparing eqn. (
3.2.22
) and eqn. (
3.2.25
). Quantities not shown, e.g.
P^{1,1}
and
P^{2,2}
, do not appear in the matching conditions at this order, hence we do not learn about any new contributions to them. Let us also comment that since
P^{1,2}=0
in the free theory limit,
P^{1,2}
being required to be nonzero serves as a probe of the effect of interactions.

Parity-odd scalar exchange:

At leading order, the exchange of a parity-odd scalar contributes to the anomalous dimension of the double-twist families as

\displaystyle\gamma_{[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}={}2\frac{\left((\delta hP)^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{-}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}'^3\rightarrow f_\phi,\;f_{\cO}^4\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\tau\rightarrow\tau_s}}}{\left(P^{2,2}\right)_{\mathcal{O}_{\tau,\ell}^{+}}\evaluated{}_{\substack{f_{\cO}^1\rightarrow f_\mathbb{1},\;f_{\cO}^2\rightarrow 0,\;l\rightarrow 0,\tau\rightarrow 0}}}=\frac{f_{\phi}^{2}}{\overline{h}^{\tau_{s}+1}}\frac{2^{\tau_{s}-1}\left(\left(\frac{2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau_{s}}{2}\right)_{\frac{\tau_{s}+1}{2}}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}\left(\frac{\tau_{s}}{2}\right)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\;,

(3.2.60a)

where
f_{\phi}
is given in the more standard
\mathcal{D}
basis. Similarly,

\displaystyle\gamma_{[\psi_{\alpha}\psi^{\alpha}]_{0}}=-\gamma_{[\psi_{(\rho}\partial^{\rho}_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}}={}

\displaystyle\frac{f^{2}_{\phi}}{\overline{h}^{\tau_{s}}}\frac{2^{{\tau_{s}}}\left(\left(\frac{2\Delta_{\psi}-\tau_{s}}{2}\right)_{\frac{\tau_{s}+1}{2}}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}\left(\frac{{\tau_{s}}}{2}\right)_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)}\;.

(3.2.60b)

Comparing with eqn. (
3.2.54b
), we see that the contribution of parity-odd scalar exchange to the parity-even double-twist family
[\psi_{(\alpha}\psi_{\beta)}]_{0}
comes at the higher order
\overline{h}^{-\tau_{s}-1}
instead of
\overline{h}^{-\tau_{s}}
.

Chapter 4
Fermions in Conformal Field Theories: Non-Perturbative Corrections

4.1
Perturbative vs Non-perturbative

In section
3
, we computed CFT data for fermions using large spin expansion. While large spin expansions has been historically useful and are numerically shown to provide surprisingly good results even at finite values of spin, there exists an alternative method which makes far more conceptual and practical sense to utilize and we will turn to that in this section.

This alternative elegant way to calculate OPE data is by making use of the Lorentzian inversion formula
[
127
,
131
]
. In addition to providing a resummation of the
1/\ell
expansion, this formalism also allows one to compute nontrivial nonperturbative effects which are exponentially suppressed at large
\ell
but may be important at smaller values of
\ell

[
8
,
227
,
9
,
133
,
228
]
. Such effects are in fact needed in order to obtain a resummation which is analytic in
\ell
. We would like to take the opportunity to review a derivation of these effects, generalizing previous computations to different external dimensions and arbitrary
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
blocks, and also to illustrate their importance in 3d CFTs.
1
1

1

The Lorentzian inversion formula yields an analytic complete result only as long as it is used properly. As we will review, Lorentzian inversion formula relies on
inversion
of the conformal blocks — which will be made clearer below — and in practice, this translates into inversion of some other function with which the conformal block is written as an expansion over since closed form expressions for
3d
conformal blocks are not available. Now, if the chosen function provides a convergent expansion for the conformal block for the whole range
z\in[0,1]
, then the inversion formula works properly. For instance,
k_{2\overline{h}}(z)
defined in eqn. (
2.3.7b
) is such a function hence the expansion in eqn. (
2.3.7a
) with
k_{2\overline{h}}
inverted does provide the full analytic results with non-perturbative corrections. On the other hand, one can also expand conformal block as functions of
y\coloneqq\frac{z}{1-z}
and invert the monomial
y^{a}
. As this expansion is only convergent for
z\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, the result of Lorentzian inversion formula only provides the perturbative terms, same terms that would be obtained by large spin computations as large spin expansion is an asymptotic expansion determined by the region
z\sim 0
(same region
y^{a}
expansion is convergent). In practice, this reflects on the result of the inversion formula: one observes non-analytic behavior at finite spin
[
227
]
— for further details see footnote 28 of
[
133
]
.

For a 4-point function of scalars
\expectationvalue{\f_1\f_2\f_3\f_4}
, the CFT inversion formula gives the OPE data for the
s
-channel in terms of two integrals of the function
g(z,\bar{z})
:

c(h,\bar{h})=c^{t}(h,\bar{h})+(-1)^{\overline{h}-h}c^{u}(h,\bar{h})\;,

(4.1.1)

where

\displaystyle c^{t}(h,\bar{h})=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{2\bar{h}}}{4}\int\limits_{0}^{1}dzd\bar{z}\mu(z,\bar{z})g^{r,s}_{d-1-h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})\text{dDisc}_{t}[g(z,\bar{z})]\;,

(4.1.2a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2\overline{h}}\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\overline{h}+r)\Gamma(\overline{h}-r)\Gamma(\overline{h}+s)\Gamma(\overline{h}-s)}{2\pi^{2}\Gamma(2\overline{h}-1)\Gamma(2\overline{h})}\;,

(4.1.2b)

\displaystyle\mu(z,\bar{z})=

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{z-\bar{z}}{z\bar{z}}\bigg|^{d-2}\frac{\left((1-z)(1-\bar{z})\right)^{s-r}}{(z\bar{z})^{2}}\;,

(4.1.2c)

and we recall that
r=h_{1}-h_{2}
and
s=h_{3}-h_{4}
. The double discontinuity around
z=0
, which we call the
s
-channel dDisc, is defined by

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{s}[g(z,\bar{z})]=\cos\left(\pi(s-r)\right)g(z,\bar{z})-\frac{1}{2}e^{i\pi(s-r)}g(ze^{2\pi i},\bar{z})-\frac{1}{2}e^{i\pi(r-s)}g(ze^{-2\pi i},\bar{z})\;.

(4.1.3)

The
t
and
u
-channel double discontinuities are defined in the same way, except around
z=1
and
z=\infty
respectively. The term
c^{u}(h,\bar{h})
is also defined in the same way as (
4.1.2a
), but with the integration being taken from
-\infty
to
0
.

In
[
127
]
the inversion formula was written in terms of
(\Delta,\ell)
, in which case the OPE coefficients for generic
\Delta
are given by

f_{12\mathcal{O}}f_{34\mathcal{O}}=-\Res_{\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta}c(\Delta^{\prime},\ell)\;,\quad\text{for fixed $\ell$}.

(4.1.4)

Here we need to take residues of
c(h,\bar{h})
with respect to
h
at fixed
\bar{h}-h=\ell
, which will introduce some extra Jacobians as in the lightcone bootstrap. We will focus on
c^{t}
since the
u
-channel can always be found by taking
1\leftrightarrow 3
and multiplying by
(-1)^{\ell}
.

It is convenient to define a generating function for the poles of
c^{t}(h,\bar{h})
:

\displaystyle c^{t}(h,\bar{h})\bigg|_{\text{poles}}=\int\limits_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{2z}z^{-h}C^{t}(z,\bar{h})\;.

(4.1.5)

The outer integral turns powers of
z
inside
C^{t}(z,\bar{h})
into poles for
h
. Since we are interested in the low-twist data, and in particular the
n=0
double-twist operators, we study the small
z
limit of
C(z,\bar{h})
:

\displaystyle C^{t}(z,\bar{h})\approx\int\limits_{0}^{1}d\bar{z}\frac{(1-\bar{z})^{s-r}}{\overline{z}^{2}}\kappa_{2\bar{h}}k^{r,s}_{2\bar{h}}(\bar{z})\text{dDisc}_{t}\left[\frac{(z\bar{z})^{h_{1}+h_{2}}g(1-z,1-\bar{z})}{[(1-z)(1-\bar{z})]^{h_{2}+h_{3}}}\right]\;,

(4.1.6)

where we have used crossing symmetry inside the dDisc.
This is a generating function for the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
primaries with respect to
\bar{z}
and to subtract descendants along
z
we have to expand the inverted block in (
4.1.2a
) in powers of
z
.

One nice example is to consider a 4-point function of identical scalars,
\expectationvalue{\f\f\f\f}
. As in the lightcone bootstrap, terms regular and logarithmic in
z
in
C^{t}(z,\bar{h})
will correspond to corrections of OPE coefficients and scaling dimensions of the double-twist towers
[\phi\phi]_{n}
, respectively. To see this we will assume the anomalous dimensions of double-twist operators are small and expand
C^{t}(z,\bar{h})
both in
z
and the anomalous dimension:

\displaystyle C^{t}(z,\bar{h})\approx z^{2h_{\phi}}P_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})(1+\delta h_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})\log(z))+\ldots\;,

(4.1.7)

where the
\log(z)
comes from a single
t-
channel conformal block. Integrating over
z
we see the term regular in
z
becomes a single pole while the term logarithmic in
z
becomes a double pole. Some of these corrections at finite spin were recently derived in the works
[
8
,
227
,
9
,
133
,
228
]
. We will review these results and present some generalizations.

As an example, we can consider the exchange of a scalar operator
\mathcal{O}
of twist
\tau_{\mathcal{O}}=2h_{\mathcal{O}}
in the
t
-channel and use the inversion formula to extract the anomalous dimension of the
[\phi\phi]_{0}
tower. In the limit
z\rightarrow 0
, the
\log(z)
piece of the scalar block
g_{h_{\mathcal{O}},h_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-z,1-\bar{z})
is known in closed form. In a general 4-point function
\expectationvalue{\f_{1}\f_{2}\f_{3}\f_{4}}
the
t
-channel blocks develop logs when
h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}
, with the coefficient given by
2
2

2

The conformal block normalization is the same as in Eq. (
2.3.6
).

g_{h_{\mathcal{O}},h_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-z,1-\bar{z})\bigg|_{\log(z)}=-\log(z)\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})}{\Gamma(h_{1}-h_{4}+h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(-h_{1}+h_{4}+h_{\mathcal{O}})}\\
\times(1-\bar{z})^{h_{\mathcal{O}}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{h_{1}-h_{4}+h_{\cO},-h_{1}+h_{4}+h_{\cO}}{2 h_{\cO}-\frac{d-2}{2}};1-\bar{z}\biggr]\;.

(4.1.8)

Focusing for now on the case where the external scalars are the same, we can match the
\log(z)
term in the generating function, yielding the correction

\hskip-14.22636pt(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\overline{h})=-f_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}^{2}\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})}{\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}}\kappa_{2\bar{h}}\int\limits_{0}^{1}\frac{d\bar{z}}{\bar{z}^{2}}k_{2\bar{h}}(\bar{z})\\
\times\text{dDisc}_{t}\left[\left(\frac{\bar{z}}{1-\bar{z}}\right)^{2h_{\phi}}(1-\bar{z})^{h_{\mathcal{O}}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{h_\cO,h_\cO}{2h_\cO-\frac{d-2}{2}};1-\bar{z}\biggr]\right]\;.

(4.1.9)

Notice that this formula gives the product
\delta h\times P
and one still needs to compute the corrected OPE coefficients, as well as add the
u
-channel contribution (identical up to a factor
(-1)^{\bar{h}-h}
), in order to find the anomalous dimension.

Using a hypergeometric transformation, we can rewrite this as

(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\overline{h})=-f_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}^{2}\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})}{\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}}\kappa_{2\bar{h}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d\bar{z}}{\bar{z}^{2}}\left(\frac{1-\bar{z}}{\bar{z}}\right)^{-\overline{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\hb,\hb}{2\hb};-\frac{\bar{z}}{1-\bar{z}}\biggr]\\
\times\bar{z}^{-h_{\mathcal{O}}}{}_{2}F_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{h_\cO,h_\cO-\frac{d-2}{2}}{2h_\cO-\frac{d-2}{2}};-\frac{1-\bar{z}}{\bar{z}}\biggr]\text{dDisc}_{t}\left[(1-\bar{z})^{h_{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\frac{\bar{z}}{1-\bar{z}}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}}\right]\;.

(4.1.10)

Finally, we can write the hypergeometric functions as a Mellin-Barnes integral and perform the
\bar{z}
integral using the identity

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d\bar{z}}{\bar{z}(1-\bar{z})}\left(\frac{\bar{z}}{1-\bar{z}}\right)^{\alpha}=2\pi\delta(i\alpha)

(4.1.11)

to find:

\displaystyle(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\overline{h})=

\displaystyle-f_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\pi(h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi})\right)\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma\left(2h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{3}\Gamma\left(h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}\frac{\Gamma(\overline{h})^{2}}{\Gamma(2\overline{h}-1)}

(4.1.12)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}ds^{\prime}\Bigg(\frac{\Gamma\left(h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}+1+s^{\prime}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\overline{h}-h_{\mathcal{O}}+2h_{\phi}-1-s^{\prime}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\overline{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}+1+s^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\left(h_{\mathcal{O}}+s^{\prime}\right)\Gamma\left(h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}+s^{\prime}\right)\Gamma(-s^{\prime})}{\Gamma\left(2h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}+s^{\prime}\right)}\Bigg)\;.

Finally, summing over the poles of
\Gamma(-s^{\prime})
gives

(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\overline{h})\big|_{\text{pert}}=-f_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}^{2}\sin^{2}(\pi(h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}))\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}+1)^{2}}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}}\\
\times\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h})^{2}\Gamma(\bar{h}-h_{\mathcal{O}}+2h_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}+1)}{}_{4}F_{3}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{h_{\cO},h_{\cO}-\frac{d-2}{2},h_{\cO}-2 h_{\f}+1,h_{\cO}-2 h_{\f}+1}{-\bar{h}+h_{\cO}-2 h_{\f}+2,\bar{h}+h_{\cO}-2 h_{\f}+1,2 h_{\cO}-\frac{d-2}{2}};1\biggr]\;,

(4.1.13)

which when expanded at large
\bar{h}
reproduces the perturbative
1/\bar{h}
expansion from the lightcone bootstrap. It can also be obtained by expanding the hypergeometric function inside Eq. (
4.1.10
) at small
\bar{z}
, performing the integrals term-by-term, and resumming the result. However, this resummation contains spurious poles in
\bar{h}
, leading to a non-analytic function, connected to the fact that performing the small
\bar{z}
expansion inside the integral fails to correctly capture its behavior near
\bar{z}\sim 1
as noted in footnote
1
.

Additionally summing over the poles of
\Gamma\left(\bar{h}-h_{\mathcal{O}}+2h_{\phi}-1-s^{\prime}\right)
gives the correction

\displaystyle(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})\big|_{\text{nonpert}}=

\displaystyle-f_{\phi\phi\mathcal{O}}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\pi(h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi})\right)\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma\left(2h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{3}\Gamma\left(h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h})^{4}}{\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)\Gamma(2\bar{h})}

(4.1.14)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(-\bar{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\phi}+1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+2h_{\phi}-1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+2h_{\phi}-\frac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}+2h_{\phi}-\frac{d}{2})}

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}F_{3}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\bar{h}, \bar{h}, \bar{h} + 2 h_{\f}-1,\bar{h}+2 h_{\f}-\frac{d}{2}}{2 \bar{h},\bar{h}-h_{\cO}+2 h_{\f},\bar{h}+h_{\cO}+2 h_{\f}-\frac{d}{2}};1\biggr]\;.

The full sum
(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})=(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})\big|_{\text{pert}}+(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})\big|_{\text{nonpert}}
has no spurious poles, and the same asymptotics as
(\delta hP)_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})\big|_{\text{pert}}
, since

\displaystyle\left(\delta h_{[\phi\phi]_{0}}(\bar{h})\right)_{\text{nonpert}}\sim 4^{-\bar{h}}\bar{h}^{1/2-4h_{\phi}}

(4.1.15)

is exponentially damped at asymptotically large
\bar{h}
. Such exponentially damped contributions can be understood as arising from the region of integration near
\bar{z}\sim 1
, while the perturbative contributions come from expanding the integrand near
\bar{z}\sim 0
.

We will now generalize the log matching to different external dimensions by considering the 4-point function
\expectationvalue{\f_1\f_2\f_2\f_1}
. The
s
-channel OPE data is given by integrating over the
t
- and
u
-channel double discontinuities, so the anomalous dimensions are given by

\left(\delta hP\right)_{[\phi_{1}\phi_{2}]_{0}}(\overline{h})=f_{11\mathcal{O}}f_{22\mathcal{O}}\left(\delta hP\right)_{1221}(\overline{h})+f_{12\mathcal{O}}^{2}\left(\delta hP\right)_{1212}(\overline{h})\;,

(4.1.16)

where

\displaystyle\left(\delta hP\right)_{1234}(\overline{h})\bigg|_{\text{pert}}=-\sin(\pi(h_{1}+h_{4}-h_{\cO}))\sin(\pi(h_{2}+h_{3}-h_{\cO}))

(4.1.17)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{2}-h_{3}+1)\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{1}-h_{4}+1)}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}+h_{2}-h_{3})\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{2}+h_{3})}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{1}-h_{2})\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{3}-h_{4})\Gamma(\bar{h}-h_{\mathcal{O}}+h_{2}+h_{4}-1)}{\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{2}-h_{4}+1)}

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}F_{3}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{h_{\cO}-h_{2}+h_{3},h_{\cO}-h_{2}+h_{3}-\frac{d-2}{2},h_{\cO}-h_{2}-h_{3}+1,h_{\cO}-h_{1}-h_{4}+1}{-\bar{h}+h_{\cO}-h_{2}-h_{4}+2,\bar{h}+h_{\cO}-h_{2}-h_{4}+1,2 h_{\cO}-\frac{d-2}{2}};1\biggr]\;,

\displaystyle\left(\delta hP\right)_{1234}(\overline{h})

\displaystyle\bigg|_{\text{nonpert}}=-\sin(\pi(h_{1}+h_{4}-h_{\cO}))\sin(\pi(h_{2}+h_{3}-h_{\cO}))

(4.1.18)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma\left(2h_{\mathcal{O}}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}+h_{2}-h_{3})\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{2}+h_{3})^{2}\Gamma\left(h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{2}+h_{3}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{1}-h_{2})\Gamma(\bar{h}-h_{1}+h_{2})\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{3}-h_{4})\Gamma(\bar{h}-h_{3}+h_{4})}{\Gamma(2\bar{h})\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(-\bar{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{2}-h_{4}+1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{1}+h_{2}-1)\Gamma\left(\bar{h}+h_{1}+h_{2}-\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\bar{h}+h_{\mathcal{O}}+h_{2}+h_{4}-\frac{d}{2})}

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}F_{3}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\bar{h}-h_{1}+h_{2},\bar{h}-h_{3}+h_{4},\bar{h}+h_{1}+h_{2}-1,\bar{h}+h_{1}+h_{2}-\frac{d}{2}}{2 \bar{h},\bar{h}-h_{\cO}+h_{2}+h_{4},\bar{h}+h_{\cO}+h_{2}+h_{4}-\frac{d}{2}};1\biggr]\;.

To derive these expressions from the inversion formula, we had to set
h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}
so the
u
- and
t
-channel blocks have
\log(z)
terms, but we left this equality implicit in the above expression.

Corrections to OPE coefficients can be derived in a similar way, by matching regular terms in
t
-channel conformal blocks. Somewhat cumbersome formulas for such corrections in general dimension and for general spin exchange were given in
[
228
]
. In section
4.2
we will describe an alternate and perhaps simpler approach to obtaining anomalous dimensions and OPE coefficient corrections in 3d CFTs, via dimensional reduction.

4.2
A detour: effect of non-perturbative terms in scalar CFTs

Ising CFT

To demonstrate why nonperturbative corrections can be important, we would like to see how they affect analytic predictions for the 3d Ising CFT. We will restrict ourselves to the 4-point function
\expectationvalue{\s\s\s\s}
and extract predictions for the
[\sigma\sigma]_{0}
scaling dimensions and OPE coefficients. We will improve the results found in
[
115
,
126
]
.

For the Ising CFT we will focus on the effects of three operators, the identity operator
\mathbb{1}
, the lightest parity-even scalar
\epsilon
, and the stress-tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
. We will also use the following results from the numerical bootstrap
[
46
,
126
]
as inputs:

\displaystyle h_{\sigma}{}

\displaystyle={}0.25907445(50)\;,\qquad h_{\epsilon}{}={}0.7063125(50)\;,\qquad h_{T}{}={}0.5\;,

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle f_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}{}={}1.0518537(41)\;,\qquad f_{\sigma\sigma T}{}={}0.32613776(45)\;.

To use the inversion formula, we will use dimensional reduction to write the 3d blocks as sums of 2d blocks
[
215
]
. Specifically, we use the expansion
3
3

3

Our parametrization differs from
[
215
]
, so
\mathcal{A}^{\text{here}}_{n,j}=\mathcal{A}^{\text{there}}_{\frac{n+j}{2},\bar{h}-h+j-n}
, and our normalization is such that
c^{(d)}_{\ell}=\frac{(d-2)_{\ell}}{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)_{\ell}}
in Eq. (2.35) of
[
215
]
.

\displaystyle g^{r,s,(3d)}_{h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\ \sum\limits_{\tiny j=\max(-n,n-\ell)}^{n}\mathcal{A}^{r,s}_{n,j}(h,\bar{h})g^{r,s,(2d)}_{h+n,\bar{h}+j}(z,\bar{z})\;,

(4.2.2a)

\displaystyle g^{r,s,(2d)}_{h,\bar{h}}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\delta_{\bar{h}-h,0}}\left(z^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h+r,h+s,2h,z)\bar{z}^{\bar{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}(\bar{h}+r,\bar{h}+s,2\bar{h},\bar{z})+(z\leftrightarrow\bar{z})\right)\;.

(4.2.2b)

In
[
215
]
this expansion was derived in closed form for
r=s=0
, which will be sufficient for our calculations.

Since each 2d block is a sum of hypergeometrics, we can use the same techniques as when inverting a single scalar block in the previous section. Specifically, after setting
r=s=0
and extracting the leading
z\rightarrow 0
behavior of the hypergeometrics in the
t
-channel, we have contributions from
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
blocks of the form

\displaystyle g^{(2d)}_{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-z,1-\bar{z})\bigg|_{z\rightarrow 0}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\delta_{\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{\mathcal{O}},0}}\left[-\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})}{\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}}(\log(z)+2\psi^{(0)}(h_{\mathcal{O}})+2\gamma)+\ldots\right]

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle\times(1-z)^{h_{\mathcal{O}}}k_{2\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-\bar{z})+(h_{\mathcal{O}}\leftrightarrow\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})\;.

Here
\psi^{(0)}(z)=\Gamma^{\prime}(z)/\Gamma(z)
is the digamma function and
\gamma
is the Euler constant. We then we find the following corrections to the OPE coefficients and anomalous dimensions after inverting the
t
-channel block:

\displaystyle\left(\delta hP\right)^{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})

\displaystyle\bigg|_{\text{pert}}=-f_{\sigma\sigma\mathcal{O}}^{2}\sin^{2}(\pi(2h_{\sigma}-\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}))

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\sigma}+1)^{2}}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h})^{2}}{\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)}\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h}-\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}+2h_{\sigma}-1)}{\Gamma(\bar{h}+\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\sigma}+1)}

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}F_{3}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\bar{h}_{\cO},\bar{h}_{\cO},\bar{h}_{\cO}-2 h_{\s}+1,\bar{h}_{\cO}-2 h_{\s}+1}{2 \bar{h}_{\cO},-\bar{h}+\bar{h}_{\cO}-2 h_{\s}+2,\bar{h}+\bar{h}_{\cO}-2 h_{\s}+1};1\biggr]\;,

(4.2.4a)

\displaystyle\left(\delta hP\right)^{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})

\displaystyle\bigg|_{\text{nonpert}}=-f_{\sigma\sigma\mathcal{O}}^{2}\sin^{2}(\pi(2h_{\sigma}-\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}))

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(2h_{\mathcal{O}})\Gamma(2\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})}{\pi^{2}\Gamma(h_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}\Gamma(\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(\bar{h})^{4}}{\Gamma(2\bar{h}-1)\Gamma(2\bar{h})}\frac{\Gamma(-\bar{h}+\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}-2h_{\sigma}+1)\Gamma(\bar{h}+2h_{\sigma}-1)^{2}}{\Gamma(\bar{h}+\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}+2h_{\sigma}-1)}

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}F_{3}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\bar{h},\bar{h},\bar{h}+2 h_{\s}-1,\bar{h}+2 h_{\s}-1}{2 \bar{h},\bar{h}-\bar{h}_{\cO}+2 h_{\s},\bar{h}+\bar{h}_{\cO}+2 h_{\s}-1};1\biggr]\;,

(4.2.4b)

with the net contribution from a given 2d block in both the
t-
and
u
-channels given by

\displaystyle(\delta hP)_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1+(-1)^{\bar{h}-h}}{1+\delta_{\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{\mathcal{O}},0}}\left[\left(\delta hP\right)^{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})\bigg|_{\text{pert}}+\left(\delta hP\right)^{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})\bigg|_{\text{nonpert}}\right]

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle+(h_{\mathcal{O}}\leftrightarrow\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})\;.

Figure 4.1:
Spectrum for
[\sigma\sigma]_{0}
in the Ising CFT derived using the inversion formula, asymptotic lightcone expansion, and numerical bootstrap. Numerical data is taken from
[
126
]
. The curves in this and later plots are obtained by matching at
z=.1
.

Similarly, by matching regular terms we obtain the corrections to the OPE coefficients

\displaystyle\delta P_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1+(-1)^{\bar{h}-h}}{1+\delta_{\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}-h_{\mathcal{O}},0}}\left(2\psi^{(0)}(h_{\mathcal{O}})+2\gamma\right)\left[\left(\delta hP\right)^{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})\bigg|_{\text{pert}}+\left(\delta hP\right)^{h_{\mathcal{O}},\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}}_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})\bigg|_{\text{nonpert}}\right]

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle+(h_{\mathcal{O}}\leftrightarrow\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}})\;.

Note that if we take
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{1}
to be the identity operator and take the limit
h_{\mathcal{O}}=\bar{h}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow 0
(as well as set
f_{\sigma\sigma\mathbb{1}}=1
), then we reproduce the expected identity contribution
P_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}=(1+(-1)^{\bar{h}-h})S_{-2h_{\sigma}}(\bar{h})
.

At finite spin and finite anomalous dimensions one does not expect that it is sufficient to match the terms logarithmic and regular in
z
to obtain the precise OPE data. Although inverting individual operators produces factors of
z^{h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{n,\ell}}}
and
z^{h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{n,\ell}}}\log z
, we know the exact generating function
C^{t}(z,\bar{h})
at small
z
is
[
127
]
:

\displaystyle C^{t}(z,\bar{h})=C_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})z^{2h_{\sigma}+\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})}+...\text{ ,}

(4.2.7)

where we ignore terms subleading in
z
.

We can then extract the anomalous dimension via:

\displaystyle\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})=\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{(z\partial_{z}-2h_{\sigma})C^{t}(z,\bar{h})}{C^{t}(z,\bar{h})}\;,

(4.2.8)

which we in practice evaluate by evaluating the generating function at small but finite
z
. We find the OPE coefficients in a similar way by taking our value for
\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})
and using:

\displaystyle C_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})=\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{C^{t}(z,\bar{h})}{z^{2h_{\sigma}+\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})}}\;,

(4.2.9)

where we once again evaluate the right-hand side at small but finite
z
.

In evaluating these expressions one wishes to take
z
small, but not too small so as to avoid neglected terms with higher powers of
\log z
from becoming important. In
[
126
]
it was found that
z=.1
is a good choice for the Ising model (there called
\bar{y}_{0}
), so we will present results at this value in our analysis. It may be helpful to further optimize the matching value of
z
. As more operators are included one should also see that the results become less and less sensitive to this choice.

Now the procedure should be clear: we can expand the 3d blocks as sums of 2d blocks and invert each block term by term. This procedure is sufficient to extract finite-spin data from the Lorentzian inversion formula. In practice we find that we need to expand to at most 10 to 15 orders in the 2d expansion such that the errors introduced by truncating this expansion are smaller than the errors from the numerical input.

Figure 4.2:
OPE coefficients
f_{\sigma\sigma[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}
in the Ising CFT. Numerical data is taken from
[
126
]
and the OPE coefficients are normalized by dividing by the mean field theory OPE coefficients.

With this data and the above expressions, we can extract
P_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}
and
\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}
, but we have to do a little more work to extract the physical OPE coefficients and scaling dimensions. To find the scaling dimensions, we need to solve the equation

\displaystyle\bar{h}-2h_{\sigma}-\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})=\ell\;,

(4.2.10)

where
\ell
is the spin of the local, double-twist operator. As the anomalous dimensions are expressed in terms of
{}_{4}F_{3}
hypergeometric functions, we will solve this equation numerically. We then calculate the physical OPE coefficients
f_{\sigma\sigma[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}
using the relation

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\sigma\sigma[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}\approx\left(1-\frac{\partial\delta h_{[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}(\bar{h})}{\partial\bar{h}}\right)^{-1}P_{\sigma\sigma[\sigma\sigma]_{0}}\;,

(4.2.11)

where the Jacobian appears because we need to take residues of the OPE function
c(\Delta,\ell)
in terms of
\Delta
at fixed spin
\ell
.

A comparison of results from the numerical bootstrap
[
126
]
, the leading asymptotic lightcone bootstrap (
3.2.11a
,
3.2.12
), and the inversion formula result, can be found in Table
4.1
. We focus here on how accurately we can reproduce the low-spin data. We see that in all cases, including the nonperturbative effects from the inversion formula leads to more accurate results. This is clearest for the scaling dimensions, where we have at least an extra digit of precision for the lightest spin-
2
and spin-
4
operators.

This improvement is especially marked for the stress-tensor and gives additional evidence that the stress-tensor should be thought of as a double-twist operator composed of two
\sigma
operators. We see a similar improvement for the OPE coefficients, although it is smaller in comparison to the dimensions. The errors listed come from the errors in the numerical input and do not include errors from truncating the operator product expansion to include only a few light operators. As we include more operators beyond
\epsilon
and
T^{\mu\nu}
we expect the results to improve even further.

or using the naïve

matching valid for perturbative anomalous dimensions. Approximate errors come from numerical input.

O(2) model

Figure 4.3:
Spectrum of
[\phi\phi]_{0}^{(I)}
and
[\phi\phi]_{0}^{(A)}
in the O(2) model. The black dots corresponds to the stress tensor and conserved current which have twist one.

We can repeat the above analysis, but now for the O(2) vector model. We will study the 4-point function of fundamental scalars
\expectationvalue{\f^{i}\f^{j}\f^{k}\f^{\ell}}
, which we can decompose in terms of the exchanged global symmetry representations as

x_{12}^{2\Delta_{\phi}}x_{34}^{2\Delta_{\phi}}\expectationvalue{\f_{i}\f_{j}\f_{k}\f_{l}}=\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}I(u,v)+(\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell})A(u,v)\\
+\left(\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell}-\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}\right)S(u,v)\;,

(4.2.12)

where
I
,
A
, and
S
correspond to contributions from exchanged operators that transform in the singlet, antisymmetric, and symmetric traceless representation of O(2), respectively.

If we collect them into a vector
\vec{Z}(u,v)=\{I(u,v),A(u,v),S(u,v)\}
, then
(1,i)\leftrightarrow(3,k)
crossing implies

\displaystyle\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)^{\Delta_{\phi}}\vec{Z}(u,v)=M\cdot\vec{Z}(u,v)\;,

(4.2.13a)

for

\displaystyle M=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&1\\
\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}&-1\\
\frac{1}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}&0\end{array}\right).

(4.2.13b)

We will use the following results from the numerical bootstrap
[
61
,
68
,
66
]
:

\displaystyle h_{\phi}

\displaystyle=0.25963(16)\;,

\displaystyle h_{\phi^{2}}

\displaystyle=0.7559(13)\;,

(4.2.14a)

\displaystyle h_{t}

\displaystyle=0.6179(16)\;,

\displaystyle f_{\phi\phi\phi^{2}}

\displaystyle=0.68726(65)\;,

(4.2.14b)

\displaystyle f_{\phi\phi J}

\displaystyle=0.52558(46)\;,\quad

\displaystyle f_{\phi\phi T}

\displaystyle=0.23146(16)\;.

(4.2.14c)

Here
t
refers to the lightest symmetric, traceless scalar in the
\phi_{i}\times\phi_{j}
OPE. There is one crucial piece of OPE data missing, the OPE coefficient
f_{\phi\phi t}
, although there are estimates from the
\epsilon
-expansion
[
195
]
, which yield

\displaystyle f_{\phi\phi t}

\displaystyle\approx\{0.8944,\,0.8246,\,0.8850\}\quad\text{at}\quad\{\mathcal{O}(\epsilon),\,\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}),\,\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\}.

(4.2.15)

Figure 4.4:
OPE coefficients for
f_{\phi\phi[\phi\phi]^{(I)}_{0}}
and
f_{\phi\phi[\phi\phi]^{(A)}_{0}}
in the O(2) model. Numerical data is taken from
[
61
]
and the OPE coefficients are normalized by dividing by the mean field theory OPE coefficients.

Using this data as input, we can calculate the low-spin spectrum for the O(2) vector model using either the asymptotic lightcone bootstrap or the inversion formula. In our calculations we expanded to
12^{th}
and
20^{th}
order in the 2d conformal blocks to obtain converged results for the stress-tensor and conserved current OPE data, respectively. The results are shown in Table
4.2
.

We see that the inversion formula in general gives more accurate results for both the conserved current
J^{\mu}
and the stress-tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
. The improvement is particularly large for
c_{\phi\phi[\phi\phi]^{A}_{0,1}}
, or the coupling between two scalars and
J^{\mu}
. One reason the inversion formula gives an improved estimate for this OPE coefficient is because it also gives a much more accurate result for
\tau_{[\phi\phi]^{(A)}_{0,1}}
, which is used as input in the calculation of the OPE coefficient. Finally, we should note that the inversion formula is only guaranteed to hold for spin
J>1
, but we see for the O(2) vector model it likely holds down to at least
J=1
.

The one exception appears to be the twist of the stress-tensor itself, for which the lightcone analysis gives a result which is slightly closer to the exact answer. We expect this is an artifact of truncating the
t
-channel expansion: as we include more operators the results for the twist will decrease which will push the lightcone result further from the exact result.

).

We can also take a different point of view and use the inversion formula to make a prediction for
f_{\phi\phi t}
. For example, if we require that the inversion formula reproduces the exact twist of
T^{\mu\nu}
then we find the following range:

\displaystyle\qquad f_{\phi\phi t}\in(0.857,0.951)\,,

(4.2.16)

with a central value of approximately
f_{\phi\phi t}=0.9038
. Using results from Monte Carlo
[
229
]
as input, setting
h_{\phi}=0.259525(50)
,
h_{\phi^{2}}=0.75562(11)
, and
h_{t}=0.6180(5),
4
4

4

[
68
]
with the Monte Carlo allowed region.
and repeating the above analysis, the window shrinks to

\displaystyle\qquad f_{\phi\phi t}\in(0.883,0.901)\,.

(4.2.17)

By including more operators in the inversion formula or the effects of operator mixing it should be possible to improve the above results further. It will be interesting to understand which operators need to be included in order to reproduce the current beyond the
10^{-3}
level. It would also be interesting to extend this work to higher orders in the small-
z
expansion to understand the higher-twist families.

4.3
Review: harmonic analysis on the conformal group

In section
4.1
we compared large spin expansions and the inversion formula and stated that the inversion formula is far more elegant both conceptually and in practical computations; furthermore, it allows us to reach non-perturbative corrections with which the analytic results behave far better at finite values of spin.
