We demonstrated this by carrying out the computations via inversion formula for 3d Ising and
\mathrm{O}(2)
models and compared those results with numerics and large spin expansions in section
4.2
. In the rest of the thesis, we will extend the application of inversion formula to CFTs with fermionic operators.

Our strategy to study the inversion formula for spinning operators involves a combination of Euclidean and Lorentzian ingredients. Our starting point for relating the fermionic
6j
symbol to the scalar one involves their Euclidean definition as an overlap of partial waves. Then we use weight-shifting operators
[
152
]
, which transform in a finite-dimensional representation of the conformal group, to expand the fermionic
6j
symbol as a sum over scalar symbols. We can plug in the explicit form of the scalar
6j
symbol as calculated via the Lorentzian inversion formula to obtain the fermionic
6j
symbol in closed form.
5
5

5

The full
6j
symbol is only known in
d=1,2,4
but the poles and residues are computable in general dimensions.
Finally, since a partial wave for general external operators can be split as a sum over two blocks, there exists a similar split for the
6j
symbol in terms of the inversion of two blocks. By splitting the scalar and fermionic
6j
symbols, we then find the inversion of a single block when we have external fermions.

In this section, we will review the harmonic analysis.

4.3.1
Shadow transform and shadow coefficients

In eqn. (
3.1.25
) we introduced the shadow transformation
\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}]
as an application of the conformally-invariant pairing given in eqn. (
3.1.23
). We remind the reader that in our conventions it reads as

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}](x)\equiv\int dy\mathcal{O}(y)\expectationvalue{\tl\cO(y)\tl\cO(x)}.

(4.3.1)

As
\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}]
is in the same conformal representation as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
, we expect that the tree point structures of one can be expanded about those of the other one: we define the
shadow matrix
as the transformation matrix between these two basis, i.e.

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\mathbf{S}[\cO_3]}^{a}=S^{a}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}[\mathcal{O}_{3}])\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\widetilde{\cO}_{3}}^{c}.

(4.3.2)

One nice way to understand such relations is through the diagrammatic notation introduced in
[
10
]
. In this language, one denotes two point functions as

\displaystyle\includegraphics[scale]{two_pt},

(4.3.3)

where we see that taking the shadow is equivalent to changing the direction of arrow. Likewise, pairing operators is gluing the arrows; for example the diagrammatic equation

\displaystyle\includegraphics[scale]{shadow_normalization}

(4.3.4)

stands for

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cdots\mathbf{S}^{2}[\cO](x)\cdots}=\int dxdy\expectationvalue{\cdots\cO(x)\cdots}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO(x)\tl\cO(y)}\expectationvalue{\cO(y)\cO(z)}=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{O}}\expectationvalue{\cdots\cO(z)\cdots},

(4.3.5)

or
\mathbf{S}^{2}[\mathcal{O}]=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}
. This follows from the definition of the shadow transformation in eqn. (
3.1.25
) and the irreducibility of the representations. The factor
\mathcal{N}
in our conventions is

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\Delta,\ell}=\frac{\pi^{3}\tan(\pi\left(\Delta+l\right) )}{(\Delta-\frac{3}{2})(-\Delta+\ell+2)(\Delta+\ell-1)}\;.

(4.3.6)

In the diagrammatic language, three point structures are denoted by three arrows connected by a node with an additional label for the basis index. For example, one can see eqn. (
4.3.2
) in Figure. (
4.5
).

Figure 4.5:
Diagrammatic definition of shadow coefficients. Note that
a
and
c
label the three-point structures, and arrows allow us to keep track of scaling dimensions. We use the standard convention where an operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,J}
with an outgoing arrow from a three-point structure enters that structure as itself. On the contrary, changing the direction of arrow is equivalent to changing
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,J}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta,J}\equiv\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Delta},J}\equiv\mathcal{O}_{3-\Delta,J}
.

One can compute shadow matrices by either in position space through the action of the weight shifting operators, or in Fourier space where the shadow transformation turns into a simple convolution
[
10
]
. Either way, we will not go into the details and simply provide the final form of the shadow matrices in our conventions for the relevant three point structures in this thesis:

\displaystyle S^{1}_{2}([\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}]\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=

\displaystyle-\frac{i\pi^{3/2}\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{5}{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta+\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{7}{2}\right)\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{7}{2}-\Delta_{\psi}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta+\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)},

(4.3.7)

\displaystyle S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}[\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\sin\left(\pi\Delta_{\phi}\right)\Gamma\left(2\left(\Delta_{\phi}-2\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{5}{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta-\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{5}{2}\right)\right)}{2^{2\Delta_{\phi}-5}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta+\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta+\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)},

\displaystyle S^{1}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell+1}\Gamma(\Delta-1)\Gamma(\ell+\Delta-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta+\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{7}{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta-\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{5}{2}\right)\right)}{\pi^{-3/2}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(\ell-\Delta+3)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta+\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)},

\displaystyle S^{1}_{3}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=

\displaystyle\frac{i\left(-\Delta+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{1}-1\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+3\right)\right)}{2\pi^{-3/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{7}{2}-\Delta_{1}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+1\right)\right)},

\displaystyle S^{1}_{3}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=

\displaystyle S^{1}_{3}([\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}),

\displaystyle S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{3/2}(-1)^{\ell}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{3}{2}\right)\Gamma(\ell+\Delta-1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+3\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell-\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+3\right)\right)}{\Gamma(\Delta-1)\Gamma(\ell-\Delta+3)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\right)\right)},

where we can get all other nonzero components from the relations

\displaystyle S^{1}_{2}([\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}]\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=-S^{2}_{1}([\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}]\phi_{-\Delta_{\phi}}\mathcal{O}_{-\Delta,\ell}),

(4.3.8)

\displaystyle S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}[\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=S^{2}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}[\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell-1}),

\displaystyle S^{1}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{\psi}}\phi_{\Delta_{\phi}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])=-S^{2}_{1}(\psi_{-\Delta_{\psi}}\phi_{-\Delta_{\phi}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}]),

\displaystyle\frac{S^{1}_{4}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{\ell}{\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2}}=\frac{S^{2}_{3}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{-\Delta+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\ell-1}{2\left(\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2\right)}}=\frac{S^{2}_{4}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{-\frac{-\Delta+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\ell-1}{2\left(\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2\right)}}=S^{1}_{3}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}),

\displaystyle\frac{S^{3}_{1}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{-\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\ell+2}{\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-2}}=\frac{S^{3}_{2}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{2\ell}{\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-2}}=\frac{S^{4}_{1}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{-\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\ell+2}{\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-2}}=\frac{S^{4}_{2}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{-\frac{2\left(\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-1\right)}{\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-2}}=S^{1}_{3}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\psi_{-\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{-\Delta,\ell}),

\displaystyle\frac{S^{1}_{4}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{-\frac{\ell}{\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2}}=\frac{S^{2}_{3}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{-\Delta+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\ell-1}{2\left(\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2\right)}}=\frac{S^{2}_{4}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{-\Delta+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\ell-1}{2\left(\Delta-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+2\right)}}=S^{1}_{3}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}),

\displaystyle\frac{S^{3}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{-\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+\ell+2}{\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2}}=\frac{S^{3}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{2\ell}{\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2}}=\frac{S^{4}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\ell-2}{\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2}}=\frac{S^{4}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})}{\frac{2\left(\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1\right)}{\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2}}=S^{1}_{3}(\psi_{-\Delta_{1}}[\psi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{-\Delta,\ell}),

\displaystyle\frac{S^{2}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])}{-\frac{2\Delta-3}{2(\Delta-1)}}=\frac{S^{2}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])}{-\frac{\Delta-2}{\Delta-1}}=S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}]),

\displaystyle\frac{S^{3}_{3}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])}{(\Delta-1)\left(\Delta+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-2\right)\left(\Delta-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)-(\Delta-2)\ell^{2}-(\Delta-2)\ell}=\frac{S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{-\Delta_{1}}\psi_{-\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta+1,\ell}])}{2(2\Delta-3)(-\Delta+\ell+2)(\Delta+\ell-1)},

\displaystyle\frac{S^{4}_{4}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])}{-(\Delta-2)\left((\Delta-1)^{2}-\Delta_{1}^{2}-\Delta_{2}^{2}+2\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\right)+(\Delta-1)\ell^{2}+\Delta\ell-\ell}=\frac{S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{-\Delta_{1}}\psi_{-\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta+1,\ell}])}{2(2\Delta-3)(-\Delta+\ell+2)(\Delta+\ell-1)},

\displaystyle\frac{S^{3}_{4}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])}{\left(2\Delta-3\right)\left(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}\right)\ell}=\frac{S^{4}_{3}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}])}{(2\Delta-3)\left(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\right)(\ell+1)}=\frac{S^{1}_{1}(\psi_{-\Delta_{1}}\psi_{-\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta+1,\ell}])}{2(2\Delta-3)(-\Delta+\ell+2)(\Delta+\ell-1)}.

The block (anti-)diagonal form of shadow matrices reflects the property that shadow transformation is parity-definite and that we have chosen our three-point structures with definite parity. As the two point function in eqn. (
3.1.25
) carries a definite parity, the shadow matrix relates the same (opposite) parity structures if the shadowed operator is of integer (half-integer) spin; this is why, say,
S^{a}_{b}([\psi]\psi\mathcal{O})
is block anti-diagonal whereas
S^{a}_{b}(\psi\psi[\mathcal{O}])
is block diagonal.
6
6

6

We remind the reader that what we refer to here as parity is simply the inversion
X_{i}\rightarrow-X_{i}
in embedding space.

4.3.2
Euclidean pairings

In this section and the rest of the thesis, we will denote conformally invariant pairings of correlation functions as
\left(\expectationvalue{\dots},\expectationvalue{\dots}\right)
which is defined as

\Big(\expectationvalue{\cO_1(x_1)\cdots\cO_n(x_n)}\;,\;\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_{\pi_1}(x_{\pi_1})\cdots\tl\cO_{\pi_n}(x_{\pi_n})}\Big)\\
\coloneqq\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}\cdots d^{d}x_{n}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1))}\expectationvalue{\cO_1(x_1)\cdots\cO_n(x_n)}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_{\pi_1}(x_{\pi_1})\cdots\tl\cO_{\pi_n}(x_{\pi_n})}

(4.3.9)

for any permutation
\pi
. We remind the reader that suppressed indices of any operator and its dual are contracted from southwest to northeast; for instance,

\left(\expectationvalue{\f_1(x_1)\f_2(x_2)\cO_3(x_3)},\expectationvalue{\tl\f_1(x_1)\tl\f_2(x_2)\tl\cO_3(x_3)}\right)\\
=\int\frac{d^{3}x_{1}d^{3}x_{2}d^{3}x_{3}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(4,1))}\expectationvalue{\f_1(x_1)\f_2(x_2)(\cO_3)_{\a_1\dots\a_{2l_3}}(x_3)}\expectationvalue{\tl\f_1(x_1)\tl\f_2(x_2)(\tl\cO_3)^{\a_1\dots\a_{2l_3}}(x_3)}\;.

(4.3.10)

Two Point Pairings and Plancherel Measure

Let us first consider the pairing of scalar two-point functions.We denote it as

\displaystyle{}\left(\expectationvalue{\phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)},\expectationvalue{\tl\phi(x_1)\tl\phi(x_2)}\right)=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}d^{d}x_{2}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1))}\expectationvalue{\phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)}\expectationvalue{\tl\phi(x_1)\tl\phi(x_2)}

(4.3.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{d}\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(1,1))\times\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d))}\expectationvalue{\phi(0)\phi(\infty)}\expectationvalue{\tl\phi(0)\tl\phi(\infty)},

where
\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(1,1))\times\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d))
is the stabilizer group for two points and the factor
2^{d}
is the Fadeev-Popov determinant.

We define an operator at infinity as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\infty)\equiv\lim\limits_{L\rightarrow\infty}L^{2\Delta}\mathcal{O}(\hat{e}L)

(4.3.12)

for a unit vector
\hat{e}
hence
\expectationvalue{\tl\phi(0)\tl\phi(\infty)}=1
in our conventions, meaning

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)},\expectationvalue{\tl\phi(x_1)\tl\phi(x_2)}\right)=\frac{1}{64\pi^{2}\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(1,1))}\;.

(4.3.13)

We can use this result to compute the pairing of spinning two point functions. To do this, we first rewrite the two-point function
\expectationvalue{\cO\cO}^{\Delta,J}
in terms of weight-shifting operators
\mathcal{D}^{a,b}
acting on
\expectationvalue{\cO\cO}^{\Delta-a,J-b}
,
7
7

7

We review these operators in section
.
then integrate it by parts, and finally act with the adjoint weight-shifting operators
\left(\mathcal{D}^{a,b}\right)^{*}\propto\mathcal{D}^{a,-b}
on the other two-point function. Diagrammatically,

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{two_point_pairing}\end{aligned}\;.

We can find the coefficient between the first two diagrams above by direct calculation. For example, if we choose
a=b=\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{\Delta,J}=-\frac{i\mathcal{D}^{(+,+)}_{1A}\mathcal{D}^{(+,+)A}_{2}}{16\left(\Delta-2\right)\left(\Delta-\frac{3}{2}\right)\left(\Delta+J-2\right)\left(\Delta+J-1\right)}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{\Delta-\frac{1}{2},J-\frac{1}{2}},

(4.3.14)

where we can integrate by parts and carry these differential operators to the other two-point function using the relation

\left(\mathcal{D}^{+,+}_{\alpha}\mathcal{O}^{\Delta-\frac{1}{2},J-\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}^{3-\Delta,J}\right)=\left(\mathcal{O}^{\Delta-\frac{1}{2},J-\frac{1}{2}},\left(\mathcal{D}^{+,+}_{\alpha}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}^{3-\Delta,J}\right)\\
=-\frac{1}{2J}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\Delta-\frac{1}{2},J-\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{D}^{+,-}_{\alpha}\mathcal{O}^{3-\Delta,J}\right).

(4.3.15)

Carrying out the computation, we find that

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{\Delta,J},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{3-\Delta,J}\right)=\frac{2J+1}{2J}\left(\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{\Delta,J-\frac{1}{2}},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{3-\Delta,J-\frac{1}{2}}\right).

(4.3.16)

Note that this recursion relation is independent of which weight-shifting operator we choose: we get exactly the same relation for all
a,b=\pm\frac{1}{2}
choices.

Using eqn. (
4.3.13
), we get the final result

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{\Delta,J},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{3-\Delta,J}\right)=\frac{2J+1}{64\pi^{2}\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(1,1))}\;.

(4.3.17)

We can use this expression to compute the Plancherel measure
8
8

8

Plancherel measure of a locally compact group
G
describes the decomposition of the irreducible unitary representations (IUR) into regular representations and are defined on the set of IUR.
for the conformal group. It is easy to see this diagrammatically:

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{Plancherel_measure}\end{aligned}\;,

where we first make use of
\mathbf{S}^{2}=\mathcal{N}
to convert the pairing into a circle and then identify the circle as the Plancherel measure up to the volume factor; see
[
10
]
for details. Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=\frac{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(1,1))}{\mathcal{N}_{\Delta,\ell}}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{\Delta,\ell}\cdot\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}^{3-\Delta,\ell}\;,

(4.3.18)

and we compute it as

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell})=\frac{(2\Delta-3)(-\Delta+\ell+2)(\Delta+\ell-1)\Gamma(2\ell+2)\cot(\pi(\Delta+l))}{128\pi^{5}}\;.

(4.3.19)

Three Point Pairings

In our conventions we have

\left(\expectationvalue{\phi_1\phi_2\cO},\expectationvalue{\tl\phi_1\tl\phi_2\tl\cO}\right)={}(-2)^{J}\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}d^{d}x_{2}d^{d}x_{3}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1))}\expectationvalue{\phi_1(x_1)\phi_2(x_2)\cO_{\mu_1\dots\mu_J}(x_3)}\\
\times\expectationvalue{\tl\phi_1(x_1)\tl\phi_2(x_2)\tl\cO^{\mu_1\dots\mu_J}(x_3)}\;,

(4.3.20)

which can be calculated by gauge fixing

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\phi_1\phi_2\cO_J},\expectationvalue{\tl\phi_1\tl\phi_2\tl\cO_J}\right)=\frac{(-1)^{J}\widehat{C}_{J}(1)}{2^{d-J}\;\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d-1))}\;,

(4.3.21)

where
2^{d}
is the appropriate Fadeev-Popov determinant. In
3d
this reads as
9
9

9

We have the convention
\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(n))=\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(n-1))\mathrm{vo\ell}(S^{n-1})
with
\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(2))=2\pi
. As what really matters is only the ratios of group volumes, this choice does not affect any physical result.

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\phi_1\phi_2\cO_J},\expectationvalue{\tl\phi_1\tl\phi_2\tl\cO_J}\right)=\frac{(-1)^{J}\Gamma\left(J+1\right)}{16\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma\left(J+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\;.

(4.3.22)

The pairing of spinning three-point functions can be calculated by reducing them via the weight-shifting operators and using the scalar pairing above. Schematically,

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{three_point_pairing}\end{aligned}\;.

The procedure to calculate the matrix
M^{ac}
is as follows. We first expand the
\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\cO}^{a}
and
\expectationvalue{\psi\phi\cO}^{a}
three-point functions in terms of
\expectationvalue{\phi\phi\cO}
, schematically

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{spinning_decomposition}\end{aligned}\;.

We then integrate by parts and act with the adjoint of these weight-shifting operators on the other spinning three-point function, which produces
\expectationvalue{\phi\phi\cO}
up to overall coefficients.
10
10

10

In
d=3
, there actually exists a compact expression for three point pairings of operators of any spin in
[
10
]
, thus one can bypass this computation.
By this procedure, we find that

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\psi_1^{\Delta_1}\phi_2^{\Delta_2}\cO^{\Delta,J}}^{m},\expectationvalue{\psi_1^{3-\Delta_1}\phi_2^{3-\Delta_2}\cO^{3-\Delta,J}}^{n}\right)={}

\displaystyle{}\frac{(-1)^{J-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(J+\frac{3}{2}\right)}{16\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(J+1)}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

(4.3.23a)

\displaystyle\left(\expectationvalue{\psi_1^{\Delta_1}\psi_2^{\Delta_2}\cO^{\Delta,J}}^{m},\expectationvalue{\psi_1^{3-\Delta_1}\psi_2^{3-\Delta_2}\cO^{3-\Delta,J}}^{n}\right)={}

\displaystyle{}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{(-1)^{J}\Gamma(J+1)}{8\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma\left(J+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}-1&\frac{1}{2}&0&0\\
\frac{1}{2}&-\frac{2J+1}{4J}&0&0\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&-\frac{J+1}{J}\\
\end{array}\right)&&J>0\\
&\frac{1}{8\pi}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}&&J=0\end{aligned}\right.

(4.3.23b)

4.3.3
Euclidean inversion formula

An
n-
point correlator can be expanded as a tensor product of two irreducible representations of the Euclidean conformal group, which basically provides us with an integral representation of a higher-point correlator in terms of lower point ones. This has been known for almost half a century since the early work of Dobrev et. al.
[
230
]
and was revived in recent years
[
231
,
127
,
10
,
132
]
. In the notation of
[
132
]
, we can schematically write

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cdots\cO_n}=\int d\mathcal{O}\int d^{d}x\expectationvalue{\cO_1(x_1)\cO_2(x_2)\cO(x)}^{a}P^{a}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{3},\dots x_{n};x)

(4.3.24)

for a generic
n-
point correlator. This corresponds to the following diagram
11
11

11

For notational brevity, we denote the matrix inverse of the pairings of three point structures as pairings in denominator, i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\expectationvalue{ABC}^{a},\expectationvalue{\tl A\tl B\tl C}^{b}\right)}{\left(\expectationvalue{ABC}^{c},\expectationvalue{\tl A\tl B\tl C}^{a}\right)}=\frac{\left(\expectationvalue{ABC}^{b},\expectationvalue{\tl A\tl B\tl C}^{a}\right)}{\left(\expectationvalue{ABC}^{a},\expectationvalue{\tl A\tl B\tl C}^{c}\right)}=\delta_{bc}\;.

(4.3.25)

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{n_point_partial_wave_expansion}\end{aligned}\;,

(4.3.26)

where we identify

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{old_style_partial_wave}\end{aligned}\;.

(4.3.27)

Here the integration measure is
d\mathcal{O}=2\pi\Delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta\left(\nu-\nu^{\prime}\right)
and it is defined over the Euclidean principal series

\displaystyle\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\nu,\quad\nu\geq 0,\qquad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.3.28)

We are glossing over the details in this quick review and refer the reader to
[
10
,
132
]
for more details.

Let us consider this general expression in the case of
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}
. For four-point functions, we can decompose
P^{a}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{3},x_{4};x)
in terms of three-point structures:

\displaystyle P^{a}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{3},x_{4};x)=\rho^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})\expectationvalue{\cO_3(x_3)\cO_4(x_4)\tl\cO(x)}^{b}.

(4.3.29)

Here
\rho^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})
are partial wave expansion coefficients and are related to OPE coefficients via eqn. (
) as we will see below. With eqn. (
4.3.31
), we can use the equation above to obtain the partial wave expansion
12
12

12

In some papers
P^{a}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{3},\dots,x_{n};x)
is referred to as conformal partial wave as well. We will not be using these objects in this paper and will reserve this term for
\Psi^{ab}_{\mathcal{O}}
defined in eqn. (
4.3.31
).
of four-point function:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}=\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4}+\int_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathcal{O}\rho^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{i})\;,

(4.3.30)

where we define the s-channel conformal partial wave as the gluing of two three-point functions

\displaystyle\Psi^{(s)ac}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{i})=\int d^{d}x\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO(x)}^{a}\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\tl\cO(x)}^{c}=\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{partial_wave_definition}.\end{aligned}\;.

(4.3.31)

Figure 4.6:
We can take the definition of the OPE function
\rho
to be the coefficients
of the pairing between a four-point correlator
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}
and a three-point structure
\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\cO}^{c}
in the basis of the three point structures
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO}^{a}
. Note that the overall coefficient also depends on the bubble coefficient
\mathcal{B}
which is a calculable kinematic term. By pairing both sides with
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\tl\cO}^{e}
, we can reduce this relation to the more standard definition generally used in the literature, such as (2.33) of
[
133
]
, (2.40) of
[
10
]
, or (1.6) of
[
131
]
. Note that these references use different conventions so the formulas are not entirely the same.

We would like to note two points about eqn. (
4.3.30
). The first point is the fact that we are explicitly writing the identity contribution because the identity block is actually orthogonal to the partial waves, hence it cannot be expanded in terms of them
[
127
]
. It is further argued in
[
131
]
that there may be other non-normalizable contributions to the four-point function that need to be written out explicitly. In particular, any scalar operator with
\Delta<\frac{d}{2}
gives such a contribution. We will assume that either there is no scalar with
\Delta<\frac{d}{2}
in the spectrum of the theory or that their contributions can be obtained by analytic continuation from the principal series.

The second point we would like to draw attention is the integration in eqn. (
4.3.30
): we specified that the integration is over the contour
C
which we define as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathcal{O}\equiv\sum\limits_{J_{\mathcal{O}}}\int\limits_{\frac{d}{2}}^{\frac{d}{2}+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}}{2\pi i}\;,\quad\int_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}d\mathcal{O}\equiv\sum\limits_{J_{\mathcal{O}}}\int\limits_{\frac{d}{2}-i\infty}^{\frac{d}{2}+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}}{2\pi i}

(4.3.32)

for convenience.
13
13

13

To be precise, in the measure
d\mathcal{O}
we now have a sum over either integer or half-integer spin, depending on the four-point function — in general dimensions
d
we have to sum over all allowed
\mathrm{SO}(d)
representations. In odd dimensions we can also have the discrete series of
\Delta
, but they will be canceled by poles in
\rho^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})
, so we will not include them explicitly.
Also, note that we give the expansion in terms of s-channel partial waves. This is indicated by the explicit
(s)
superscript on
\rho
and
\Psi
. Additionally, we leave the dependence of
\rho
and
\psi
on external operators implicit.

Figure 4.7:
Diagrammatic illustration of the s-channel partial wave expansion of the four-point function, assuming that the identity contribution is the only non-normalizable contribution. Instead of separating it, we can deform it onto the principal series and deform back after the analytic continuation from principal series to physical poles.

The definition of
\rho
in eqn. (
4.3.29
) is diagrammatically shown in figure
4.6
. We can pair both sides with a three-point function and obtain the Euclidean inversion formula:

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O}_{5})=\frac{\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}...d^{d}x_{5}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1))}\mu(\mathcal{O}_{5})\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\tl\cO_5}^{c}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_3\tl\cO_4\cO_5}^{d}}{\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\tl\cO_5}^{a},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_5}^{c}\right)\left(\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\tl\cO_5}^{d},\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_3\tl\cO_4\cO_5}^{b}\right)}\;.

(4.3.33)

Note that we can rewrite this as
14
14

14

Remember that suppressed indices are contracted and they always go from southwest to northeast, hence the order of correlators are important in our conventions, i.e.
\expectationvalue{\dots}\expectationvalue{\cdots}=(-1)^{\#}\expectationvalue{\cdots}\expectationvalue{\dots}
.

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})=\frac{\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}...d^{d}x_{4}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1))}\left(\int d^{d}x_{5}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\tl\cO_5}^{c}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_3\tl\cO_4\cO_5}^{d}\right)\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}}{\frac{(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}}{\mu(\mathcal{O}_{5})}\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\tl\cO_5}^{a},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_5}^{c}\right)\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_3\tl\cO_4\cO}^{b},\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\tl\cO_5}^{d}\right)}\;,

(4.3.34)

for the shorthand notation

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ij}=\left\{\begin{aligned} -1&&\text{if }\mathcal{O}_{i}\text{ and }\mathcal{O}_{j}\text{ are both fermions}\\
1&&\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.

(4.3.35)

As we will show below, the “denominator” above is actually the inverse of the
partial wave normalization
hence we conclude

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})=\eta_{(ac)(bd)}^{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}cd}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}(x_{i}),\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}\right).

(4.3.36)

where
\eta_{(ac)(bd)}^{\mathcal{O}}\eta^{(ce)(df)}_{\mathcal{O}}=\delta^{e}_{a}\delta^{f}_{b}
for
\eta^{(ce)(df)}_{\mathcal{O}}
given in eqn. (
4.3.44
).
15
15

15

In the rest of the thesis, we use the shorthand notation
\Psi^{\widetilde{(\chi)}ab}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{i})
which is defined as
\Psi^{\widetilde{(\chi)}ab}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{i})=\Psi^{(\chi)ab}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{i})\lvert_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{O}_{4}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{4}}
for any channel
\chi=s,t,u
.

We could have derived this result by starting from the partial wave expansion of figure
4.7
, pairing it with
\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}cd}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}
, and utilizing the orthogonality of the partial waves directly.
16
16

16

When we pair the partial wave expansion eqn. (
4.3.30
) with a partial wave of the same channel, there is actually another term coming from the pairing of identity exchange with the partial wave. However the pairing of identity exchange with the partial wave of the same channel is proportional to a tadpole diagram:

\begin{aligned} \includegraphics[scale]{tadpole}\end{aligned}\;.

(4.3.37)

Such diagrams are zero by the irreducibility of the representations unless
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{1}
, which is never the case for the partial waves on the principal series.

We would like to remind the reader that the diagrams, albeit useful, are to be considered as schematic expressions only. In particular, they are agnostic to possible signs associated to the orderings of fermions. As an example, consider eqn. (
4.3.26
): in our conventions it stands for

\hskip-14.45377pt\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cdots\cO_n}=\int d\mathcal{O}\mu(\mathcal{O})\int d^{d}xd^{d}x_{1}^{\prime}d^{d}x_{2}^{\prime}\frac{\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO(x)}^{a}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO(x)\tl\cO'_{2}\tl\cO'_{1}}^{c}\expectationvalue{\cO'_{1}\cO'_{2}\cO_3\cdots\cO_n}}{\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO\tl\cO_{2}\tl\cO_{1}}^{a},\expectationvalue{\cO_{1}\cO_{2}\cO}^{c}\right)}\;,

(4.3.38a)

but not

\hskip-14.45377pt\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cdots\cO_n}\neq\int d\mathcal{O}\mu(\mathcal{O})\int d^{d}xd^{d}x_{1}^{\prime}d^{d}x_{2}^{\prime}\frac{\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO(x)}^{a}\expectationvalue{\cO'_{1}\cO'_{2}\cO_3\cdots\cO_n}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO(x)\tl\cO'_{2}\tl\cO'_{1}}^{c}}{\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO\tl\cO_{2}\tl\cO_{1}}^{a},\expectationvalue{\cO_{1}\cO_{2}\cO}^{c}\right)}\;.

(4.3.38b)

However, one cannot deduce this from the diagram alone.

Bubble Coefficients and Partial Wave Normalization

One of the interesting pairings that we can consider is
the so-called bubble integral
17
17

17

This follows from the irreducibility of representations.

\expectationvalue{\cdots\tl\cO'(x)\cdots}\int d^{d}x_{1}d^{d}x_{2}\expectationvalue{\cO_{1}(x_1)\cO_{2}(x_2)\cO'(x)}^{a}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_{1}(x_1)\tl\cO_{2}(x_2)\tl\cO(y)}^{b}\\
=\delta_{\mathcal{O}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}\delta^{d}(x-y)\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2};\mathcal{O}}^{ab}\expectationvalue{\cdots\tl\cO(y)\cdots},

(4.3.39)

which we can see in figure
4.8
in diagrammatic language. By imposing
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}
and taking the trace of both sides without acting on
\expectationvalue{\cdots\tl\cO'(x)\cdots}
, we can relate the bubble coefficient
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2};\mathcal{O}}^{ab}
to the three point pairing and the Plancherel measure:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2};\mathcal{O}}^{ab}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\expectationvalue{\cO_{1}\cO_{2}\cO}^{a},\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_{1}\tl\cO_{2}\tl\cO}^{b}\right)}{{P\ell anchere\ell measureoftheconforma\ell group}}.

(4.3.40)

Figure 4.8:
Definition of the bubble annihilation matrix
\mathcal{B}
. One can explicitly calculate
\mathcal{B}
by removing the gray blob above and connecting both ends: this relates
\mathcal{B}
times the Plancherel measure to the pairing of two three-point functions, which can then be computed by going to a fixed conformal frame and carrying out the explicit calculations. A similar calculation is carried out in
4d
in
[
10
]
, see appendix C there. Here we repeated it for
3d
.

One straightforward usage of these bubble matrices is the calculation of the normalization of the conformal partial wave defined in eqn. (
4.3.31
):

\displaystyle\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}},\Psi^{(s)cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)=\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}...d^{d}x_{6}}{\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1)}\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\cO_5}^{a}\cdot\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_3\tl\cO_4\widetilde{\cO}_5}^{b}\cdot\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_6}^{c}\cdot\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\widetilde{\cO}_6}^{d}\;,

(4.3.41)

If we perform the
x_{3,4}
integrals, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}},\Psi^{(s)cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)=\delta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}}(-1)^{\Sigma_{66}}\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{4};\mathcal{O}_{6}}^{bd}\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\tl\cO_6}^{a},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_6}^{c}\right),

(4.3.42)

where
\delta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}}=\delta_{\ell_{5}\ell_{6}}2\pi\delta(\nu_{5}-\nu_{6})
for
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\nu
and where
(-1)^{\Sigma_{66}}
follows from the change of the order of the three-point functions. Next we use eqn. (
4.3.40
) to find the more symmetric form:

\displaystyle\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}},\Psi^{(s)cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)=\delta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}}\eta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}^{(ac)(bd)}

(4.3.43)

for

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}^{(ac)(bd)}\coloneqq\frac{(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}}{\mu(\mathcal{O}_{5})}\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_1\tl\cO_2\tl\cO_{5}}^{a}\cdot\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_{5}}^{c}\right)\left(\expectationvalue{\tl\cO_3\tl\cO_4\cO_{5}}^{b}\cdot\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\tl\cO_{5}}^{d}\right)\;.

(4.3.44)

Note that changing the order of the three-point functions brings an overall sign
(-1)^{2(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+\ell_{4})}=1
.
18
18

18

That this term is 1 follows from Lorentz invariance as we need an even number of fermions in a non-zero vacuum expectation value.
Since we also have
\mu\left(\mathcal{O}\right)=\mu(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}})
, the partial wave normalization satisfies the following symmetries:

\displaystyle\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}ab}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},\Psi^{(s)cd}_{\mathcal{O}}\right)=\left(\Psi^{(s)cd}_{\mathcal{O}},\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}ab}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}\right),

(4.3.45a)

\displaystyle\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}ab}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},\Psi^{(s)cd}_{\mathcal{O}}\right)=\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}cd}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}}\right).

(4.3.45b)

4.3.4
Partial Waves and Conformal Blocks

In this section we will briefly review the relation between the conformal partial wave expansion and the conformal block expansion. The goal is to establish the general dictionary between the two for general four-point functions. The method we use is not new, but it will be useful to present the results in our conventions, taking care of signs with fermionic operators.

First we recall that our definition for the partial wave and shadow transform are:

\displaystyle\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}x_{5}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_5}^{a}\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\widetilde{\cO}_{5}}^{b}\;,

(4.3.46)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}](x)

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}y\mathcal{O}(y)\expectationvalue{\cO(y)\cO(x)}\;.

(4.3.47)

We will also find it useful to define the kinematic functions
C
:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}}\expectationvalue{\cO_1(x_1)\cO_2(x_2)\cO_5(x_5)}^{a}\sim C^{a}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{12})\expectationvalue{\cO_2(x_2)\cO_2(x_5)}\;.

(4.3.48)

We can then define s-channel conformal blocks for
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}
as solutions to the conformal Casimir equation with the following behavior in the limit
x_{3}\rightarrow x_{4}
and
x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}
:

\displaystyle G^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})\approx C^{p}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{12})C^{q}_{\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{34})\expectationvalue{\cO_{5}(x_2)\cO_{5}(x_4)}\;.

(4.3.49)

Here we work in Euclidean space and the order of limits does not matter. With this definition the four-point function has the following conformal block expansion:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}=\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}}\lambda_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}}^{a}\lambda_{\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}}^{b}G^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}}(x_{i})\;,

(4.3.50)

for the OPE coefficients
\lambda
are defined as in eqn. (
3.1.14
).

Now we have to expand the conformal partial wave as a sum of two conformal blocks. To extract their coefficients, we just need to study the integrand in certain limits. We start by taking the limit
x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}
under the integrand in (
4.3.46
) and then performing the
x_{5}
integral. In this limit we have:

\displaystyle\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\supset\int d^{d}x_{5}C^{a}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{12})\expectationvalue{\cO_5(x_2)\cO_5(x_5)}\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_5\widetilde{\cO}_{5}(x_5)}^{b}

\displaystyle\supset C^{a}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{12})S^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4\cO_5(x_2)}^{c}\;.

(4.3.51)

To get the second line we have to reorder the operators in the two-point function and implicitly raise and lower the spinor indices, so the two possible signs cancel. Taking the
x_{3}\rightarrow x_{4}
limit we find:

\displaystyle\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\supset C^{a}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{12})S^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])C^{c}_{\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{34})(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}\expectationvalue{\cO_{5}(x_2)\cO_5(x_4)}\;.

(4.3.52)

To get the coefficient for the other block we take the limit
x_{3}\rightarrow x_{4}
under the integrand, perform the
x_{5}
integral and then take the limit
x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}
:

\displaystyle\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{I})

\displaystyle\supset S^{a}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\mathcal{O}_{5}])C^{c}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{12})C^{b}_{\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{34})(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}\expectationvalue{\cO_{5}(x_2)\cO_5(x_4)}\;.

(4.3.53)

We therefore find that the full partial wave is:

\displaystyle\Psi^{(s)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})=(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}\left[S^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])G^{(s)ac}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})+S^{a}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}[\mathcal{O}_{5}])G^{(s)cb}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}}(x_{i})\right]\;.

(4.3.54)

We can now use this equation inside eqn. (
4.3.30
) to obtain
19
19

19

There are also non-renormalizable contributions such as identity contribution, but we are dropping them here with the assumption that they can be obtained later by analytic continuation.

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathcal{O}\rho^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}\left[S^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])G^{(s)ac}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})+S^{a}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}[\mathcal{O}_{5}])G^{(s)cb}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}}(x_{i})\right]

(4.3.55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}d\mathcal{O}\rho^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})(-1)^{\Sigma_{55}}S^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])G^{(s)ac}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})

where in the second line we used the shadow symmetry of the integrand and the definitions of the contours, i.e. eqn. (
4.3.32
). By closing the contour to the right and comparing with eqn. (
4.3.50
), we find the following relation between the OPE function and the OPE coefficients:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}}^{a}\lambda_{\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}}^{b}=(-1)^{1+\Sigma_{55}}\text{Res}_{\Delta=\Delta_{5}}\rho^{(s)}_{ac}S^{c}_{b}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])\bigg|_{J=J_{5}}\;,

(4.3.56)

where we first set
J=J_{5}
and then evaluate the residue.

4.3.5
6j
Symbols

In eqn. (
4.3.56
), we found the relation to compute the OPE coefficients from the Euclidean inversion formula. We can now ask the general question: what does the contribution of a single operator
\mathcal{O}
in the t-channel map to in the s-channel under crossing? As realized in
[
112
,
111
]
, by studying the lightcone limit, an isolated operator
\mathcal{O}
maps to double-twist operators in the crossed channel. To review this result in the current language, let us first introduce the
6j
symbol of the conformal group.

The
6j
symbol is defined as the overlap of a t- and s-channel partial wave, which for external scalars is

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{\Psi}^{(s)}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}},\Psi^{(t)}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int dx_{1}...dx_{6}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\f}_1\widetilde{\f}_2\widetilde{\cO}_5}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\f}_{3}\widetilde{\f}_{4}\cO_5}\expectationvalue{\f_3\f_2\cO_6}\expectationvalue{\f_1\f_4\widetilde{\cO}_{6}}\;.

(4.3.59)

Using the
6j
symbol it is possible to write a single t-channel partial wave as a spectral integral over s-channel partial waves:

\displaystyle\Psi^{(t)}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}(x_{i})=\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathcal{O}_{5}\frac{1}{\eta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right\}\Psi^{(s)}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})\;,

where
\eta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}
is the normalization of partial waves of external scalars, special case of the general formula in eqn. (
4.3.44
).

In practice the
6j
symbol (
4.3.5
) has been calculated using the Lorentzian inversion formula in
d=2
and
d=4
. The Lorentzian inversion formula gives another integral representation of the OPE function, but now with the correlator integrated over a causal diamond in Minkowski space:
20
20

20

Our convention is
\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(n))=\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(n-1))\mathrm{vo\ell}(S^{n-1})
.

\displaystyle\rho^{(s)}(\Delta,J)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\Delta,J}\int\limits_{0}^{1}\int\limits_{0}^{1}dzd\bar{z}\mu(z,\bar{z})G^{(s)}_{J+d-1,\Delta+1-d}(z,\bar{z})\frac{\expectationvalue{[\f_3,\f_2][\f_1,\f_4]}}{T^{(s)}}+(\text{u-channel})\;,

(4.3.62)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\Delta,J}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi^{d-2}\Gamma(\frac{d-2}{2})\Gamma(J+d-2)}{2^{d+J+3}\text{vol}(\text{SO}(d-1))\Gamma(d-2)\Gamma(J+\frac{d-2}{2})}\frac{\Gamma(J+1)\Gamma(\Delta-\frac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(J+\frac{d}{2})\Gamma(\Delta-1)}

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\Delta_{12}+J+\Delta}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\Delta_{21}+J+\Delta}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\Delta_{34}+J+\widetilde{\Delta}}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\Delta_{43}+J+\widetilde{\Delta}}{2})}{\Gamma(J+\Delta)\Gamma(J+d-\Delta)}\;.

(4.3.63)

Here
T^{(s)}
is a kinematic s-channel prefactor:

\displaystyle T^{(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|x_{12}|^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}|x_{34}|^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}}\left(\frac{|x_{24}|}{|x_{14}|}\right)^{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}\left(\frac{|x_{14}|}{|x_{13}|}\right)^{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}\;.

(4.3.64)

The u-channel term is the same but with
3\leftrightarrow 4
. One can show that this form of the Lorentzian inversion formula is equivalent to the form we used above in. eqn. (
4.1.2
), where we extract by comparing eqn. (
4.3.56
) and eqn. (
4.1.4
) that
c(\Delta,\ell)=\rho^{(s)}(\Delta,\ell)S(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta,\ell}])
.

By first inverting individual blocks we can find the
6j
symbol:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=S(\phi_{3}\phi_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}])\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)+S(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}[\mathcal{O}_{6}])\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi^{\tilde{1}\tilde{2}\tilde{3}\tilde{4}}_{\Delta_{5},J_{6}},G^{3214}_{\Delta_{6},J_{6}}\right)_{L},

where the subscript
L
in (
4.3.5
) is to emphasize that we use the Lorentzian inversion formula
[
133
]
.

We do not have a closed form expression for the
6j
symbol in generic dimensions, but for
d=3
it is straightforward to calculate its poles and residues by using dimensional reduction and the explicit
d=2
expressions
[
215
,
1
]
. Let us now focus on the problem of inverting a single operator. The contribution of a single block for
\mathcal{O}_{6}
exchange gives

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\f_1\f_2\f_3\f_4}\supset\lambda_{\phi_{3}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}}\lambda_{\phi_{1}\phi_{4}\mathcal{O}_{6}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}d\mathcal{O}_{5}\frac{1}{\eta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{3}&\phi_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)S(\phi_{3}\phi_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])G^{1234}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}(x_{i})\;.

As a function of
\Delta_{5}
the integrand of (
4.3.5
) has poles at the following locations:

\displaystyle\Delta_{5}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+2n+J_{5}\;,

(4.3.75)

\displaystyle\Delta_{5}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}+2n+J_{5}\;.

(4.3.76)

These are the dimensions and spins of the double-twist operators
[\phi_{1}\phi_{2}]_{n,J}
and
[\phi_{3}\phi_{4}]_{n,J}
in mean field theory (MFT)
[
112
,
111
]
. A special case is when
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}
in which case we get single and double poles corresponding to corrections to the MFT spectrum and OPE coefficients of
[\phi_{1}\phi_{2}]_{n,J}
. An important exception is when we are inverting the identity block,
\mathcal{O}_{6}=\mathbb{1}
, in which case we get single poles and find the MFT OPE coefficients themselves. We will work out the MFT OPE coefficients for fermions in section
4.4
.

We will now extend this discussion to non-scalar external operators. To keep track of possible signs associated with fermionic operators, let us proceed step by step. For spacelike seperated operators, we have the relation
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}=(-1)^{\Sigma_{23}+\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{13}}\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_2\cO_1\cO_4}
, with which eqn. (
4.3.36
) reads

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O}_{5})=(-1)^{\Sigma_{23}+\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{13}}\eta_{(ac)(bd)}^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}cd}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}}(x_{i}),\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_2\cO_1\cO_4}\right).

(4.3.77)

If we now apply the partial wave expansion in eqn. (
4.3.30
) we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O}_{5})=(-1)^{\Sigma_{23}+\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{13}}\eta_{(ac)(bd)}^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}\int_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathcal{O}_{6}\rho^{(t)}_{ef}(\mathcal{O}_{6})\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}cd}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}}(x_{i}),\Psi^{(t)ef}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}(x_{i})\right).

(4.3.78)

We identify the conformally invariant pairing above as the generalization of
6j
symbol in eqn. (
4.3.5
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right\}^{abcd}=\left(\widetilde{\Psi}^{(s),ab}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}},\Psi^{(t),cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)=\int d^{d}x_{1}...d^{d}x_{6}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_1\widetilde{\cO}_2\widetilde{\cO}_{5}}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_3\widetilde{\cO}_4\cO_5}\\
\times\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_2\cO_6}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_4\widetilde{\cO}_{6}}.

(4.3.79)

As in the scalar case we can split this into two pieces, corresponding to the inversion of individual blocks:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right\}^{abcd}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\Sigma_{66}}\bigg[S^{d}_{e}\left(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}]\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)^{abce}

\displaystyle\hskip 54.2025pt+S^{c}_{e}\left(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{2}[\mathcal{O}_{6}]\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)^{abed}\bigg]\;,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)^{abcd}

\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{\Psi}^{(s),ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}},G^{(t),cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)_{L}\;.

By using the shadow symmetry of the integrand, we can then rewrite eqn. (
4.3.78
) as

\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O}_{5})=(-1)^{\Sigma_{66}+\Sigma_{23}+\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{13}}\eta_{(ac)(bd)}^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}\\
\times\int_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}d\mathcal{O}_{6}\rho^{(t)}_{ef}(\mathcal{O}_{6})S^{f}_{g}\left(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}]\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{array}\right)^{cdeg}\;.

(4.3.88)

If we now close the contour
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
to the right and pick up the residues at
\Delta=\Delta_{6}
, multiply both sides with
S^{c}_{b}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}])
, and then pick up the residues of both sides at
\Delta=\Delta_{5}
, we obtain the formula for the correction due to the inversion of a single block
G^{(t),fg}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}
in the crossed channel:

\lambda_{125,a}\lambda_{345,b}\bigg|_{G^{(t),fg}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}=(-1)^{1+\Sigma_{55}+\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{13}+\Sigma_{23}}\lambda_{326,f}\lambda_{146,g}\\
\times\Res_{\Delta=\Delta_{5}}\eta_{(ad)(ce)}^{\Delta,J}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{O}_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{2}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}&\mathcal{O}_{4}&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,J}\end{array}\right)^{defg}S^{c}_{b}(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}])\bigg|_{J=J_{5}}\;,

(4.3.89)

where we use eqn. (
4.3.56
) both in
s
and
t
channels.

The problem now is how to reduce the full
6j
symbol, (
4.3.79
), to a sum of scalar
6j
symbols, (
4.3.5
). To do this we will need to use weight-shifting operators, which allow us to reduce general conformal integrals involving fermions to those involving scalars. We will review these operators in the next section.

4.3.6
Weight-Shifting Operators

The work
[
152
]
introduced differential operators,
\mathcal{D}^{A}
, which transform in a finite-dimensional representation of the conformal group
\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1)
given by the index
A
. By acting with weight-shifting operators we can transform a conformally-invariant tensor structure involving an operator
\mathcal{O}
to a conformally covariant structure involving a new operator
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
. Here, we will use weight-shifting operators which change the spin by half-integers.

In
d=3
the double cover of the conformal group
\mathrm{SO}(3,2)
is
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
, so we will use weight-shifting operators which transform in the fundamental representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. We will use the notation of
[
152
]
and write the four possible operators as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\pm\pm}:[\Delta,\ell]\rightarrow[\Delta\pm\frac{1}{2},\ell\pm\frac{1}{2}]\;,

(4.3.90)

which in embedding space are given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}^{-+}\right)_{a}=

\displaystyle S_{a}\;,

(4.3.91a)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}^{--}\right)_{a}=

\displaystyle X_{ab}\frac{\partial}{\partial S_{b}}\;,

(4.3.91b)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}^{++}\right)_{a}=

\displaystyle-2(\Delta-1)S_{b}(\partial_{X})^{b}_{\;\;a}-S_{a}S_{b}(\partial_{X})^{b}_{\;\;c}\frac{\partial}{\partial S_{c}}\;,

(4.3.91c)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}^{+-}\right)_{a}=

\displaystyle 4(1+\ell-\Delta)(\Delta-1)\Omega_{ab}\frac{\partial}{\partial S_{b}}+2(1+\ell-\Delta)X_{ab}(\partial_{X})^{b}_{\;c}\frac{\partial}{\partial S_{c}}

\displaystyle-S_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial S_{b}}X_{bc}(\partial_{X})^{c}_{\;d}\frac{\partial}{\partial S_{d}}\;.

(4.3.91d)

In the rest of the paper, we will suppress spinor index of the weight-shifting operators.

We will use these operators to relate conformal integrals involving fermionic tensor structures to known integrals involving bosonic structures. For this, we need to define the adjoint of a weight-shifting operator with respect to our bilinear pairing:

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}\mathcal{O},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\right)=\left(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{D}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\right)\;,

(4.3.92)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{O},\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\right)=\int d^{d}x\mathcal{O}(x)\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(x)\;.

(4.3.93)

We should stress that here
\mathcal{O}
is shorthand for some representation of the conformal group and does not need to obey the spin-statistics theorem. We will define the adjoint by moving from the left to the right, where we recall there are suppressed spinor indices.

It is not hard to see that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}^{pq}\right)^{*}\bigg|_{\Delta,\ell}=\zeta^{pq}_{\ell}\mathcal{D}^{p,-q}\bigg|_{\widetilde{\Delta}\mp\frac{p}{2},\ell\pm\frac{q}{2}}\;,

(4.3.94)

where
p,q=\pm 1
and we have emphasized that the adjoint of a weight-shifting operator depends explicitly on the representation it acts on, although the coefficient
\zeta
only depends on the spin.

To calculate the adjoints in practice we go to the Poincaré section, or work in physical space. The result is summarized as:

\displaystyle\zeta^{--}_{\ell}=-2\ell\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\zeta^{-+}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\;,

(4.3.95)

\displaystyle\zeta^{+-}_{\ell}=2\ell\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\zeta^{++}_{\ell}=-\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\;.

(4.3.96)

The next ingredient we need is the crossing equation for covariant two-point functions:
21
21

21

To avoid clutter, we will use
\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\pm\pm}
to denote a weight-shifting operator acting on the
n^{\text{th}}
operator in the correlator that follows the weight-shifting operator. For example,
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{ab}\expectationvalue{\cO_{\De_1,l_1}(x_1)\cO_{\De_2,l_2}(x_2)\cO_{\De_3,l_3}(x_3)}
stands for
\mathcal{D}^{ab}(X_{2},S_{2})\expectationvalue{\cO_{\De_1,l_1}(X_1,S_1)\cO_{\De_2,l_2}(X_2,S_2)\cO_{\De_3,l_3}(X_3,S_3)}
in the embedding space.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}^{pq}\expectationvalue{\cO_{\Delta,\ell}(x_1)\cO_{\Delta+\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}(x_2)}=\alpha^{pq}_{\Delta,\ell}\mathcal{D}_{2}^{-p,-q}\expectationvalue{\cO_{\Delta,\ell}(x_1)\cO_{\Delta+\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}(x_2)}\;,

(4.3.97)

and we find

\displaystyle\alpha^{--}_{\Delta,\ell}=

\displaystyle{}\frac{i\ell}{(2\Delta-3)(\Delta+\ell-1)}\;,

(4.3.98a)

\displaystyle\alpha^{-+}_{\Delta,\ell}=

\displaystyle{}-\frac{i}{2(2\Delta-3)(2\ell+1)(-\Delta+\ell+2)}\;,

(4.3.98b)

\displaystyle\alpha^{+-}_{\Delta,\ell}=

\displaystyle{}-8i(\Delta-1)\ell(-\Delta+\ell+1)\;,

(4.3.98c)

\displaystyle\alpha^{++}_{\Delta,\ell}=

\displaystyle{}\frac{4i(\Delta-1)(\Delta+\ell)}{2\ell+1}\;.

(4.3.98d)

We can now find the shadow transform of weight-shifted operators:

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{D}^{pq}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}](x)

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}y\left(\mathcal{D}^{pq}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}(y)\right)\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_{\Delta+\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}(y)\widetilde{\cO}_{\Delta+\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}(x)}

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}y\zeta^{pq}_{\ell}\alpha^{p,-q}_{\widetilde{\Delta}-\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}(y)\mathcal{D}^{-a,b}_{2}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_{\Delta,\ell}(y)\widetilde{\cO}_{\Delta,\ell}(x)}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{pq}_{\ell}\alpha^{p,-q}_{\widetilde{\Delta}-\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}\mathcal{D}^{-pq}\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}](x)\;,

(4.3.99)

or

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{D}^{pq}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}](x)

\displaystyle=\chi^{pq}_{\Delta,\ell}\mathcal{D}^{-p,q}\mathbf{S}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta,\ell}]\;,

(4.3.100a)

\displaystyle\chi^{pq}_{\Delta,\ell}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{pq}_{\ell}\alpha^{p,-q}_{\widetilde{\Delta}-\frac{p}{2},\ell+\frac{q}{2}}\;.

(4.3.100b)

This gives a way to push the shadow transform past the weight-shifting operators. Then to calculate the shadow transform of a three-point tensor structure we will relate the fermionic structures to the bosonic ones. For simplicity we focus on three-point function structures involving one fermion and one scalar:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_{\Delta_1}\phi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_3,\ell_3}}\;,

(4.3.101)

where
\mathcal{O}
has half-integer spin. We want to use weight-shifting operators to write such a three-point function in terms of a structure involving two scalars:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\phi_{\Delta_{1}'}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\cO_{\Delta_{3}',\ell_{3}'}}\;,

(4.3.102)

where the third operator
\mathcal{O}
now has integer spin. There are two equivalent ways to do this:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_{\Delta_1}\phi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_3,\ell_3}}^{a}=\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-p,+}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{-+}\expectationvalue{\phi_{\Delta_1+\frac{p}{2}}\phi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}}},

(4.3.103a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_{\Delta_1}\phi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_3,\ell_3}}^{a}=\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{2,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-p,+}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{++}\expectationvalue{\phi_{\Delta_1+\frac{p}{2}}\phi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_{3}-\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}}},

(4.3.103b)

where each matrix,
\kappa_{1,2}
, is invertible. As the next simplest case, we can consider the three point structure involving two fermions

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_{\Delta_1}\psi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_3,\ell_3}}^{a}=\sum\limits_{p,q}\kappa^{a}_{3,pq}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-p,+}\mathcal{D}_{2}^{-q,+}\expectationvalue{\phi_{\Delta_1+\frac{p}{2}}\phi_{\Delta_2+\frac{q}{2}}\cO_{\Delta_{3}}}\;.

(4.3.104)

where now the index
a
runs from
1
to
4
hence
\kappa_{3}
is again invertible. The explicit forms of these
\kappa
matrices are easily computable though we will not present them as they are rather lengthy and do not provide any particular insight.

As we stated in section
4.3.1
, one can use weight shifting operators to find shadow matrices. To illustrate this, let us consider a few simple examples. The first one is the calculation of the matrix
S^{a}_{b}(\psi_{1}[\phi_{2}]\mathcal{O}_{3})
:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\psi_{\Delta_1}\mathbf{S}[\phi_{\Delta_2}]\cO_{\Delta_3,\ell_3}}^{a}

(4.3.105)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}_{2}\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-p,+}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{-+}\expectationvalue{\phi_{\Delta_1+\frac{p}{2}}\phi_{\Delta_2}\cO_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})S(\phi_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2}}[\phi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-p,+}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{-+}\expectationvalue{\phi_{\Delta_1+\frac{p}{2}}\phi_{\widetilde{\Delta}_2}\cO_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{p,b}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})S(\phi_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2}}[\phi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}})\left(\kappa^{-1}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\right)^{p}_{b}\expectationvalue{\psi_{\Delta_1}\phi_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}\cO_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}}}^{b}.

(4.3.106)

Here we first wrote the fermionic structure in terms of the bosonic one, then acted with the shadow transform on the simple three point function involving two scalars, and then finally acted with the weight-shifting operators. After acting with the weight-shifting operators we expressed the answer in the original basis. Therefore, the relevant shadow matrix is

\displaystyle S^{a}_{b}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}[\phi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})=\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})S(\phi_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2}}[\phi_{\Delta_{2}}]\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}})\left(\kappa^{-1}_{1}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\right)^{p}_{b}.

(4.3.107)

As the next example, let us turn to
S^{a}_{b}([\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})
. To find the shadow transform of
\psi_{\Delta_{1}}
, we now have to pass the shadow transform past the weight-shifting operators using (
4.3.100a
) and (
4.3.100
):

\displaystyle S^{a}_{b}(

\displaystyle[\psi_{\Delta_{1}}]\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})

(4.3.108)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\chi^{-p,+}_{\Delta+\frac{p}{2},0}S\left([\phi_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2}}]\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\kappa^{-1}_{1}(\psi_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\right)^{-p}_{b}.

Lastly, one needs to pass shadow transform past the weight shifting operator for the shadow transform of the
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}}
in a similar manner:

\displaystyle S^{a}_{b}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}}])=\sum\limits_{p}\kappa^{a}_{1,p}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\chi^{-,+}_{\Delta_{3}+\frac{1}{2},\ell_{3}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\kappa^{-1}_{2}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{3},\ell_{3}})\right)^{p}_{b}.

(4.3.109)

The computation of shadow matrices via weight shifting operators are analogus for three point structures of two fermions. For the sake of completeness, the shadow matrix for the shadow transform of spin
-\ell
operator reads as

\displaystyle S^{a}_{b}(\psi_{1}\psi_{2}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}}])=\sum\limits_{p,q}\kappa^{a}_{3,pq}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})S(\phi_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2}}\phi_{\Delta_{2}+\frac{q}{2}}[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}}])\left(\kappa^{-1}_{3}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{3},\ell_{3}})\right)^{pq}_{b},

(4.3.110)

whereas the expression for the shadow transform of one of the
\psi
operators is

S^{a}_{b}([\psi_{1}]\psi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}])=\sum\limits_{p,q}\kappa^{a}_{3,pq}(\psi_{\Delta_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})S([\phi_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2}}]\phi_{\Delta_{2}+\frac{q}{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\\
\times\chi^{-p,+}_{\Delta_{1}+\frac{p}{2},0}\left(\kappa^{-1}_{3}(\psi_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}\psi_{\Delta_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{3},\ell_{3}})\right)^{-p,q}_{b}.

(4.3.111)

4.4
Mean Field Theory OPE Coefficients

In mean field theory we have the factorized correlator

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3\cO_4}=\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2}\expectationvalue{\cO_3\cO_4}+(-1)^{\Sigma_{23}}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_3}\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_4}+\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_4}\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_3}\;.

(4.4.1)

The two-point function
\expectationvalue{\cO_i(x_i)\cO_j(x_j)}
is only non-zero when
\mathcal{O}_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{j}
, but we will leave this restriction implicit in this section.

We will now expand the MFT four-point function in s-channel partial waves and extract the partial wave expansion coefficient. The first term is automatically separated in the s-channel partial wave expansion, so we can focus on the latter two Wick contractions.

The
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_4}\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_3}
contraction gives

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})\bigg|_{\expectationvalue{14}\expectationvalue{23}}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}_{(ac)(bd)}\left(\widetilde{\Psi}^{(s),cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_4}\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_3}\right)

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}_{(ac)(bd)}\int\frac{d^{d}x_{1}...d^{d}x_{5}}{\mathrm{vo\ell}(\mathrm{SO}(d+1,1))}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_{1}\widetilde{\cO}_{2}\widetilde{\cO}_{5}}^{c}\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_3\widetilde{\cO}_4\cO_5}^{d}\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_4}\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_3}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}_{(ac)(bd)}(-1)^{\Sigma_{11}+\Sigma_{22}}S^{d}_{e}(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}]\mathcal{O}_{5})S^{e}_{f}([\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{5})\left(\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\cO}_{1}\widetilde{\cO}_{2}\widetilde{\cO}_{5}}^{c},\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_1\cO_5}^{f}\right)\;,

where we have made the replacements
\mathcal{O}_{3,4}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{2,1}
, respectively.

The other contraction gives a similar result:

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})\bigg|_{\expectationvalue{13}\expectationvalue{24}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\Sigma_{23}}\eta^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}_{(ac)(bd)}\left(\widetilde{\Psi}^{(s),cd}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_3}\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_4}\right)

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\Sigma_{23}+\Sigma_{11}+\Sigma_{22}}\eta^{\mathcal{O}_{5}}_{(ac)(bd)}S^{d}_{e}([\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}]\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5})S^{e}_{f}(\mathcal{O}_{1}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}]\mathcal{O}_{5})\left(\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\rangle^{c},\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_5}^{f}\right)\;.

The full result in MFT is then

\displaystyle\rho^{(s),\text{MFT}}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})=\hat{\delta}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{4}}\hat{\delta}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}}\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})\bigg|_{\expectationvalue{14}\expectationvalue{23}}+\hat{\delta}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{4}}\hat{\delta}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}}\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})\bigg|_{\expectationvalue{13}\expectationvalue{24}}\;,

(4.4.4)

where
\hat{\delta}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}}=\delta_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}}\delta_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}
and should not be confused with the delta function on the principal series. Next we will apply this explicitly to various correlators containing fermions in order to calculate their MFT coefficients.

4.4.1
\expectationvalue{\psi\phi\phi\psi}

For the correlator
\expectationvalue{\psi\phi\phi\psi}
, the identity operator only appears in the t-channel, hence eqn. (
4.4.4
) becomes

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})=

\displaystyle-\eta^{\mathcal{O}}_{(ac)(bd)}S^{d}_{e}(\widetilde{\phi}[\widetilde{\psi}]\mathcal{O})S^{e}_{f}([\widetilde{\phi}]\psi\mathcal{O})\left(\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\psi}\widetilde{\phi}\widetilde{\cO}}^{c},\expectationvalue{\phi\psi\cO}^{f}\right)\;.

(4.4.5)

By explicit calculation, we find

\rho^{(s)}_{ac}(\mathcal{O})S^{c}_{b}(\phi\psi[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}])=\frac{\pi^{5/2}(2\Delta-3)(2J+1)4^{\Delta_{\phi}-2}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{3}{2}\right)(-\Delta+J+2)(\Delta+J-1)}{\Gamma(\Delta-1)\Gamma\left(J+\frac{3}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\\
\times\frac{\Gamma(J+1)\csc\left(\pi\Delta_{\psi}\right)\sin\left(\pi\Delta_{\phi}\right)\csc\left(2\pi\Delta_{\phi}\right)\csc(\pi(J-\Delta))}{\Gamma\left(2\Delta_{\phi}-1\right)\Gamma(-J+\Delta-1)\Gamma(J+\Delta)}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}c_{1}&0\\
0&c_{2}\\
\end{array}\right)\;,

(4.4.6)

with coefficients

c_{1}=-\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\phi\psi}-1\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\psi\phi}+1\right)\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\phi\psi}+5\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\psi\phi}+7\right)\right)}\\
\times\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta_{\phi\psi\mathcal{O}}-1\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta+2\Delta_{\phi}+2\Delta_{\psi}-5\right)\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta_{\phi\psi\mathcal{O}}+5\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta-2\Delta_{\phi}-2\Delta_{\psi}+13\right)\right)}

(4.4.7a)

and

c_{2}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\phi\psi}+1\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\psi\phi}-1\right)\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\phi\psi}+7\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta_{\mathcal{O}\psi\phi}+5\right)\right)}\\
\times\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta_{\phi\psi\mathcal{O}}+1\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J+2\Delta+2\Delta_{\phi}+2\Delta_{\psi}-7\right)\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta_{\phi\psi\mathcal{O}}+7\right)\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(2J-2\Delta-2\Delta_{\phi}-2\Delta_{\psi}+11\right)\right)}\;,

(4.4.7b)

where
\Delta_{abc}\coloneqq\Delta_{a}+\Delta_{b}-\Delta_{c}
as in eqn. (
3.1.16b
).

We see that the first component has residues at
\Delta=\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}+J-\frac{1}{2}+2n
, which corresponds to the double twist families
[\phi\psi_{\alpha}]_{n,J}
. In contrast, the last component has residues at
\Delta=\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}+J+\frac{1}{2}+2n
, which corresponds to the double twist families
[\phi\partial_{\alpha\beta}\psi^{\beta}]_{n,J}
.

By taking their respective residues, we can find the OPE coefficients. For the leading (
n=0
) tower,
22
22

22

Results for higher-twist towers are given in section
4.4.4
.
they read as

\displaystyle P_{11}^{(s)}\left([\phi\psi]_{0,J}\right)=

\displaystyle-\frac{\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{\psi}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)}{\Gamma\left(J+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\phi}\right)\Gamma\left(2J+\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{3}{2}\right)}\;,

(4.4.8a)

\displaystyle P_{22}^{(s)}\left([\phi\partial\psi]_{0,J}\right)=

\displaystyle-\frac{(2J+1)2^{3-2\Delta_{\phi}}\Gamma(J+1)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{\psi}\right)\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}+J-1\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}\left(2\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)\Gamma(2J+3)\Gamma\left(2\Delta_{\psi}-2\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\phi}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(2J+2\Delta_{\phi}+2\Delta_{\psi}-3\right)}{\Gamma\left(2J+\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}\;.

(4.4.8b)

where we define shorthand notation
23
23

23

The reason for the difference between eqn. (
4.4.9
) and
P_{\mathcal{O}}^{a,b}=(-1)^{\ell}f_{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\mathcal{O}}^{a}f_{\psi_{3}\psi_{4}\mathcal{O}}^{b}
as given in eqn. (
3.2.41
) is the difference in the conformal block normalization used in this chapter and chapter
3
— also see footnote
11
.

\displaystyle P^{(s)}_{ab}(\mathcal{O})\coloneqq\lambda^{a}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}}\lambda^{b}_{\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}}\;.

(4.4.9)

At large
J
, the leading behavior is

\displaystyle P_{11}^{(s)}\left([\phi\psi]_{0,J}\right)\sim

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{\pi}2^{-\Delta_{\psi}-\Delta_{\phi}-2J+\frac{5}{2}}J^{\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}-1}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi})}\;,

(4.4.10a)

\displaystyle P_{22}^{(s)}\left([\phi\partial\psi]_{0,J}\right)\sim

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)2^{-\Delta_{\psi}-\Delta_{\phi}-2J+\frac{3}{2}}J^{\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}-2}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\phi}\right)}\;.

(4.4.10b)

As we are working with three-point structures such that
\lambda_{\phi\psi\mathcal{O},a}=(-1)^{J-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda_{\psi\phi\mathcal{O},a}
, we see that

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{\psi\phi\mathcal{O},1}\right)^{2}\sim

\displaystyle-(-1)^{J-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}2^{-\Delta_{\psi}-\Delta_{\phi}-2J+\frac{5}{2}}J^{\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}-1}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi})}\;,

(4.4.11a)

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{\psi\phi\mathcal{O},2}\right)^{2}\sim

\displaystyle-(-1)^{J-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\left(\Delta_{\psi}-1\right)2^{-\Delta_{\psi}-\Delta_{\phi}-2J+\frac{3}{2}}J^{\Delta_{\psi}+\Delta_{\phi}-2}}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\psi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{\phi}\right)}\;.

(4.4.11b)

In the free theory limit with
\{\Delta_{\phi},\Delta_{\psi}\}\rightarrow\{\frac{1}{2},1\}
, these become

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{\psi\phi\mathcal{O},1}\right)^{2}\sim-(-1)^{J-\frac{1}{2}}4^{1-J}\sqrt{\frac{J}{\pi}}\;,\quad\left(\lambda_{\psi\phi\mathcal{O},2}\right)^{2}\sim 0\;.

(4.4.12)

We see that this matches the results of
[
148
]
once we take the normalizations into account.
Specifically, they normalize their operators as

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\cO_{\Delta,\ell}(X_1,S_1)\cO_{\Delta,\ell}(X_2,S_2)}

\displaystyle=c^{\text{there}}_{\mathcal{O}}i^{2\ell}\frac{\expectationvalue{S_1S_2}^{2\ell}}{X_{12}^{2\Delta+2\ell}}\;,

(4.4.13)

\displaystyle c^{\text{there}}_{\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4^{\Delta-1}(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}-J}\Gamma\left(J+\frac{3}{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)_{J+\frac{1}{2}}}\;,

(4.4.14)

while we take
c^{\text{here}}_{\mathcal{O}}=1
.
Thus we have

\displaystyle c_{\mathcal{O}}^{\text{there}}\left(\lambda_{\psi\phi\mathcal{O},1}\right)^{2}\sim-4J\;,

(4.4.15)

which can be compared with Eq. (4.4) of
[
148
]
.

4.4.2
\expectationvalue{\psi_1\psi_2\psi_2\psi_1}

Now we turn to correlators containing four fermions. For a correlator of the form
\expectationvalue{\psi_1\psi_2\psi_2\psi_1}
, the identity operator only appears in the
14\rightarrow 23
channel, hence eqn. (
4.4.4
) becomes

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})=\eta^{\mathcal{O}}_{(ac)(bd)}S^{d}_{e}(\widetilde{\psi}_{2}[\widetilde{\psi}_{1}]\mathcal{O})S^{e}_{f}([\widetilde{\psi}_{2}]\psi_{1}\mathcal{O})\left(\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\psi}_{1}\widetilde{\psi}_{2}\widetilde{\cO}}^{c},\expectationvalue{\psi_2\psi_1\cO}^{f}\right)\;.

(4.4.16)

When we calculate
\rho
, which we will not reproduce here, we see that it is block-diagonal, with the upper
2\times 2
block having poles at
\Delta=\Delta_{\psi_{1}}+\Delta_{\psi_{2}}+J-1+2n
, and with the lower
2\times 2
block having poles at
\Delta=\Delta_{\psi_{1}}+\Delta_{\psi_{2}}+J+2n
. These correspond to the double-twist families
[\psi_{1\alpha}\psi_{2\beta}]_{n,J}
and
[\psi_{1\alpha}\psi_{2}^{\alpha}]_{n,J}
respectively. We then can read off the explicit results for OPE coefficients.

For the leading tower (
n=0
) we obtain:

\displaystyle P^{(s)}_{[\psi_{1\alpha}\psi_{2\beta}]_{0,J}}=-\frac{\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(J)\Gamma\left(2J+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

(4.4.17)

\displaystyle P^{(s)}_{[\psi_{1\alpha}\psi_{2}^{\alpha}]_{0,J}}=-\frac{\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+2J-1\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(J+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)}{4\Gamma\left(\Delta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(J+2)\Gamma\left(2J+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+J-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{2}+J-\frac{1}{2}\right)+c_{1}&\left(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\right)J(J+1)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+J-\frac{3}{2}\right)\\
-\left(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\right)J(J+1)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+J-\frac{3}{2}\right)&\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+J-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{2}+J-\frac{1}{2}\right)-c_{1}\end{pmatrix}\;,

with

c_{1}=\frac{1}{4}\left(2\Delta_{1}-1\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)\left(2\Delta_{2}-1\right)\\
+\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\right)J^{3}+\left(\Delta_{1}^{2}+\left(4\Delta_{2}-\frac{9}{2}\right)\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}^{2}-\frac{9\Delta_{2}}{2}+3\right)J^{2}\\
+\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1\right)\left(\Delta_{1}\left(2\Delta_{2}-1\right)-\Delta_{2}\right)J\;.

(4.4.18)

Results for higher-twist towers are given in section
4.4.4
.

4.4.3
\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\psi\psi}

For identical fermions
\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\psi\psi}
, both the t- and u-channels contribute, hence we have

\displaystyle\rho_{ab}^{(s)}(\mathcal{O})=

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{O}}_{(ac)(bd)}S^{d}_{e}(\widetilde{\psi}[\widetilde{\psi}]\mathcal{O})S^{e}_{f}([\widetilde{\psi}]\psi\mathcal{O})\left(\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\psi}\widetilde{\psi}\widetilde{\cO}}^{c},\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\cO}^{f}\right)

\displaystyle-\eta^{\mathcal{O}}_{(ac)(bd)}S^{d}_{e}([\widetilde{\psi}]\widetilde{\psi}\mathcal{O})S^{e}_{f}(\psi[\widetilde{\psi}]\mathcal{O})\left(\expectationvalue{\widetilde{\psi}\widetilde{\psi}\widetilde{\cO}}^{c},\expectationvalue{\psi\psi\cO}^{f}\right).

For the leading (
n=0
) tower, the explicit expressions for the OPE coefficients are as follows:

\displaystyle P^{(s)}_{[\psi_{\alpha}\psi_{\beta}]_{n,J}}=
