\scalebox{.8}{
$\mathcal{K}^{\raisebox{-1.68776pt}{\scalebox{1.8}{$\cdot$}}2}_{22}\footnotesize\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\psi_{1}&\phi_{2}&\phi_{2}&\psi_{1}&\mathcal{O}_{5}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\phi_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&\phi_{2}&\phi_{2}&\phi_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{5}^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{6}\end{array}\right)\normalsize$
}&\frac{i\left(\ell_{5}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\left(-\Delta_{6}+\ell_{6}-1\right)\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta_{6}-\ell_{6}-4\right)\right)}{\left(\Delta_{6}-1\right)\left(\ell_{5}+1\right)\ell_{6}}\end{array}

(A.3.2)

A.4
Symmetries of
6j
Symbols

Figure A.3:
Diagrammatic form of the
6j
symbol
\left(\Psi^{\widetilde{(s)}cd}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}},\Psi^{(t)ab}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)
as a tetrahedron.

By representing the
6j
symbol as a tetrahedron as in figure
A.3
, we can reveal its symmetries, as was done in
[
133
]
. Explicitly, we can consider the three transformations as the generators of the symmetry group:

S1

Rotation around the axis that passes through the vertex
a
and the center of the triangle
\Delta bcd
, generated by the permutation
(1\widetilde{5}\widetilde{4})(\widetilde{1}54)(236)(\widetilde{2}\widetilde{3}\widetilde{6})(bcd)
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdab}=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{2}\\
\mathcal{O}_{6}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{4}\end{aligned}\right\}^{dbac}\;.

(A.4.1a)

S2

Rotation around the axis that passes through the vertex
c
and the center of the triangle
\Delta abd
, generated by the permutation
(125)(\widetilde{1}\widetilde{2}\widetilde{5})(\widetilde{3}46)(3\widetilde{4}\widetilde{6})(bad)
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdab}=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}}\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{5}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{6}\mathcal{O}_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cbda}\;.

(A.4.1b)

S3

Reflection with respect to the plane that passes through the points
c
,
d
, and the midpoint of the line segment
\overline{ab}
, generated by the permutation
(12)(\widetilde{1}\widetilde{2})(34)(\widetilde{3}\widetilde{4})(6\widetilde{6})(ba)
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdab}=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdba}\;.

(A.4.1c)

The overall phases in the front follow from the fermionic nature of the correlators and can be checked explicitly.

The validity of eqn. (
A.4.1
) depends on the choice of three point basis. For example, the first two equalities require us to work in a basis which respects the cyclic permutations; i.e., we should have
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}^{a}=\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_3\cO_1}^{a}=\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_3\cO_1}^{a}
. Generically, we can always find a basis which respects this property.

The equality eqn. (
A.4.1c
) on the other hand requires the basis to respect inversions, i.e. we should have
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}^{a}=\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_1\cO_3}^{a}
. We can always choose a basis to respect this
unless
we have
\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}=\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}
. In that case, we can no longer choose two independent bases
\expectationvalue{\cO_1\cO_2\cO_3}^{a}
and
\expectationvalue{\cO_2\cO_1\cO_3}^{a}
to satisfy the required equality; we need the same basis to satisfy this condition. However, if we work in a parity definite basis, all
nonzero

6j
symbols will have an even number of parity odd three-point structures, therefore the equality holds. Assuming we are in such a basis, we can use following relations to derive all permutations:

\displaystyle\textbf{S1}^{3}=\textbf{S2}^{3}=\textbf{S3}^{2}=\textbf{E}\text{ with }(\textbf{S2}*\textbf{S1})^{2}=(\textbf{S3}*\textbf{S1})^{4}=(\textbf{S3}*\textbf{S2})^{2}=\textbf{E}

(A.4.2)

where
E
is the identity transformation.

In summary, we can derive eqn. (
A.4.1
) and similar identities by considering the inversions and rotations of the tetrahedron and are valid in a parity definite basis with a cyclic property. These conditions are trivially satisfied for external scalars as there is only one three-point structure.

An interesting set of transformations is the one that does not move the edges
5,6
. There are only three such permutations:

\displaystyle(12)(\widetilde{1}\widetilde{2})(34)(\widetilde{3}\widetilde{4})(6\widetilde{6})(ab),

(A.4.3)

\displaystyle(5\widetilde{5})(23)(\widetilde{2}\widetilde{3})(14)(\widetilde{1}\widetilde{4})(cd),

\displaystyle(13)(\widetilde{1}\widetilde{3})(24)(\widetilde{2}\widetilde{4})(5\widetilde{5})(6\widetilde{6})(ab)(cd),

which yields

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdab}=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdba}=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}}\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{dcab}\\
=(-1)^{2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}+2\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{dcba}\;.

(A.4.4)

But we also know how to relate
\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}
to
\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}
, and likewise for
\mathcal{O}_{6}
, due to shadow symmetry of the partial waves.

We may also be interested in interchanging
\mathcal{O}_{5,6}
in the
6j
symbol, and this can be achieved with the transformation
\mathfrak{T}=(1\widetilde{1})(2\widetilde{4})(3\widetilde{3})(56)(ad)(bc)
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdab}=(-1)^{2\left(\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}+\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\right)}\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\end{aligned}\right\}^{badc}.

(A.4.5)

Likewise, with the transformation
\mathfrak{T}=(1\widetilde{3})(2\widetilde{2})(4\widetilde{4})(5\widetilde{6})(ac)(bd)
, we get

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{5}\end{aligned}\right\}^{cdab}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{3}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{4}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\end{aligned}\right\}^{abcd}.

(A.4.6)
